, there is population decline, which favours the presence of dormancy. This effect, however, may not be sufficiently large to compensate the smaller proportion of mature mutant individuals by the end of the branching phase (see e.g. the plot of the fixation probability as a function of
b_{0}
when
{\xi}_{\infty}=0.9
in Figure
3
). Nonetheless, if
{\xi}_{\infty}
is sufficiently small, then we see an advantage for the seed bank trait (with respect to the evolution of a neutral trait without dormancy). Even in this scenario, the fixation probability does not necessarily decrease monotonically with
b_{0}
(see the plot for
{\xi}_{\infty}=0.8
in Figure
3
), which reflects the smaller proportion of mature mutant individuals by the end of the branching phase (see Lemma
2.2
). In this case, there exists an optimal value of
b_{0}\in(0,1)
that maximises the fixation probability.

Figure 3:
Fixation probability of
({\rho}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
for different values of
{\xi}_{\infty}\in\{0.7,0.8,0.9,1.0,1.1,1.2\}
and
b_{0}\in(0,1]
. We assume the dynamics of
({\xi}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is described by (
2.42
) with
r=20
, and that
({\rho}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
solves the SDE (
2.41
) with
K=1
, so that
\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}})
is given by (
2.21
). To take into account the impact of dormancy on the branching phase (see Lemma
2.2
), we computed the fixation probability assuming that
{\rho}_{0}(0)={\psi}_{B}(0.01)
for all
B=1-b_{0}
, where
{\psi}_{B}
is the function defined in Lemma
2.2
. Observe that in the absence of dormancy, i.e. when
b_{0}=1
, the fixation probability of a neutral trait is not affected by the changes in population size. Our numerical simulations suggest that for sufficiently small values of
{\xi}_{\infty}
, i.e. for sufficiently large declines in population size, the evolution of the seed bank trait is favoured with respect to the evolution of a neutral trait without dormancy.

2.2.2
Case
(ii)
:
{\eta}\neq 0

We now suppose that the limiting population size
({\xi}(t))_{t\geq 0}
,
is a diffusion process, satisfying

d{{\xi}}={\alpha}({\xi})dt+{\eta}({\xi})dW_{\textrm{env}}(t).

We again consider the system of SDEs corresponding to the coupled
evolution of the carrying capacity
{\xi}
and the proportion of mutants
in the population
{\rho}_{0}
from
Corollary
2.7
.

To analyse the impact of dormancy in this setting, recall the
definition of
\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}
from (
2.2
).
For
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the real-valued function

g:\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}\times[0,1]\times[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

by

\displaystyle g\Big((b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}},{\rho}_{0},{\xi}\Big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\rho}_{0}\left[\frac{B(1-{\rho}_{0})}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1){\xi}}+\frac{{\rho}_{0}}{2}\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{0}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}\right)-\frac{2B}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1)}\right)\right],

(2.43)

where the parameter
B
is the mean time spent in the seed bank
(see (
2.3
)). Observe that the integrand
inside the square brackets in the second line
of (
2.40
) is given by

{\eta}^{2}({\xi}){\xi}^{-1}g
. For positive values of
g
, fluctuations in
population size favour the dormancy trait, and for negative values
they disfavour it. The behaviour of the function
g
defined
in (
2.43
)
is not trivial since it depends on at least three parameters
(see Figure
4
).

Figure 4:
Plot of
the function
g
defined
in (
2.43
)
for
K=1
and
B=1-b_{0}\in\{0.1,\,0.5,\,1.0\}
.
The values of
g
were computed by applying (
2.21
)
to (
2.43
),
fixing different values of
{\xi}
in panels (a) (
{\xi}=0.8
) and (b) (
{\xi}=1.2
).
For large values of
B
, the function
g
becomes negative when the
proportion of mutants
{\rho}_{0}
is sufficiently high.
The threshold value of
B
for which this occurs depends on the
population size (parameter
{\xi}
);
indeed, in contrast to panel (a), in panel (b) the function
g
is
negative for
B=0.5
and
{\rho}_{0}>0.9
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
0<{\xi}_{\min}<{\xi}_{\max}
.
The map
g:\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}\times[0,1]\times[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

in (
2.43
)
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}\in\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}

and
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
, we have

g(\boldsymbol{b},0,{\xi})=g(\boldsymbol{b},1,{\xi})=0.

(ii)

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}\in\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}

and
0<{\xi}_{\min}<{\xi}_{\max}
, there exists

{\rho}_{c}={\rho}_{c}(K,\boldsymbol{b},{\xi}_{\max})\in(0,1)
such that for
all
{\rho}_{0}\in(0,{\rho}_{c})
and
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
, we have

g(\boldsymbol{b},{\rho}_{0},{\xi})>0
.

(iii)

There exists a decreasing function

{\xi}\in(0,\infty)\mapsto B_{c}({\xi})>0
satisfying

\lim_{{\xi}\rightarrow\infty}B_{c}({\xi})=0

(2.44)

such that if
K\in\mathbb{N}
and

\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}\in\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}

are chosen in such way that
B>B_{c}({\xi}_{\min})
, then there is

{\rho}_{c}={\rho}_{c}({\xi}_{\min})\in(0,1)
such that for any

{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
, we have

g(\boldsymbol{b},{\rho}_{0},{\xi})<0
for
{\rho}_{0}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
.

Biologically,
Proposition
2.8
can be interpreted as follows:

(1)

By assertion (ii), the fluctuations in the population size favour
the growth of the proportion of mutants carrying the dormancy trait when this
proportion is sufficiently low.

(2)

By assertion (iii), if the mean time to germination is too large,
the fluctuations in the population size favour the coexistence of the mutant
and wild types. This phenomenon resembles the case of fluctuating selection,
where heterozygotes are favoured when compared to homozygotes
(see e.g.
[
32
]
).

Our findings suggest that, at least within a certain range of parameters,
fluctuations in population size favour the dormancy trait. However, there
exists a critical age such that, if the mean germination
time exceeds this critical value, the wild type is favoured when a large
proportion of mutants is present, which is in agreement with the predictions
made by Cohen in
[
21
]
.

2.3
Wright-Fisher model in a fast-changing environment

In our final regime, we consider the impact of very rapid fluctuations in the
environment. We are going to assume that the total population size is
fixed, but that these fluctuations affect the ability of mature plants
to produce seed.
Let
M>0
be a large parameter. We modify the model of
Section
2.1
as follows.
Let
({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of
i.i.d. random variables taking values in
\{-1,0,1\}
,
representing annual environmental variations (such as rainfall and
temperature fluctuations
[
23
]
). In generation

t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, each wild type individual contributes

\Poiss\!\left(M\,(1+s_{N}\,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(t+1))\right)

seeds, while for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
, each mutant in
generation
t-i
contributes

\Poiss\!\left(Mb_{i}\,(1+s_{N}\,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(t+1-i))\right)

to the pool of seeds ready to germinate in generation
t+1
. Here,

s_{N}>0
controls the magnitude of environmental variation. Observe that this
model is analogous to the Wright-Fisher model with fluctuating selection
(see
[
12
, Section 3]
), with the distinction that in our
model, the total production of seeds of both wild type and mutant individuals
living in the same generation is equally affected by the environment. Thus,
as before, there is no intrinsic advantage to either carrying or not
carrying the seed bank mutation.
In the classical case of the Wright-Fisher model with fluctuating selection,
in order to obtain a diffusive limit,
the selection parameter (which is equivalent to the parameter
s_{N}
in our
model) is usually assumed to be of order
N^{-1/2}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.
Following
this strategy, we next impose analogous conditions on
s_{N}
and the sequence of
random variables
({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
that will allow
us to establish convergence of our model (after scaling) to an SDE.

Assumption 2

(Dynamics of the fast-changing environment)
.

The sequence
(s_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is such that for every

N\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{N}\in[0,1)
and there exists
s\in(0,\infty)

such that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}N^{1/2}s_{N}=s.

(2.45)

Moreover, there exists
p\in[0,1/2]
such that for every

N\in\mathbb{N}
,
({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a sequence
of i.i.d. random variables satisfying

\mathbb{P}({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(0)=-1)=\mathbb{P}({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(0)=1)=\frac{1-\mathbb{P}({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}_{0}^{N}(0)=0)}{2}=p.

(2.46)

As before,
X^{N}_{i}(t)
will denote the number of mutant individuals in
generation
t-i
for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
. We also write

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}}^{N}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{i}(t))_{i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket_{0}}
,
where
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{i}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t-i)
for

i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket_{0}
. Similarly to the case of a constant
environment, we define the Markov process

(\boldsymbol{X}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}}^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as follows.
For any
t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(i)

Given
(\boldsymbol{X}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}}^{N}(t))
,
the conditional distribution of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t+1)

follows (
2.46
).

(ii)

The conditional distribution of
X^{N}_{0}(t+1)
given

(\boldsymbol{X}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}}^{N}(t),{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t+1))
is

\textrm{Bin}\left(N,\,\frac{b_{0}(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t+1))X^{N}_{0}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{i-1}(t))X^{N}_{i}(t)}{\left(N-X^{N}_{0}(t)+b_{0}X^{N}_{0}(t)\right)(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t+1))+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{i-1}(t))X^{N}_{i}(t)}\right).

(2.47)

In (
2.47
),

(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t+1))
reflects the environmental influence
on the production of seeds by individuals in generation
t
, and

(1+s_{N}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}^{N}_{i-1}(t))
reflects the environmental
influence on seed production in generation
t-i
, for

i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
.

We will define a suitably scaled process and show that it converges to an
SDE as
N\to\infty
. The main difficulty here is the presence of three
contributions to the dynamics, corresponding respectively to evolutionary
change due to genetic drift, dormancy in the
seed bank, and the fast environmental fluctuations. The usual approach to
identifying a diffusion limit for
the Wright-Fisher model with fluctuating selection is based on an averaging
of the action of the infinitesimal generator over the environmental
fluctuations (see
[
39
,
12
]
). The
difficulty of applying this technique to our setting is that, as explained
in Sections
2.1.2

and
2.2
, it is not possible to
establish the diffusive limit in the Wright-Fisher model with seed bank
through a generator computation. However, as we shall see, Katzenberger’s
result will still apply, essentially because, much as in the case of
fluctuating selection, we choose
s_{N}
in such a way that
the amplitude of the
fast environmental fluctuations tends to zero and the
corresponding quadratic variation process
converges over evolutionary timescales.
Formally, for each

N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the process

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
by

\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}^{N}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor)}{N}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=s_{N}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 7\relax}}}}^{N}(\lfloor Nt\rfloor).

(2.48)

Let
\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the natural filtration of the
process
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.
Before stating the main convergence theorem of this section, we record the
conditional expectations of the componentwise increments of the process

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

For all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,+\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left.x^{N}_{0}\left(t+\tfrac{1}{N}\right)-x^{N}_{0}(t)\right|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]

(2.49)

\displaystyle=\frac{2ps_{N}^{2}(1-x^{N}_{0}(t))\Big((1-x^{N}_{0}(t))+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t)\Big)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)\right)}{\left[h\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t)\right)^{2}-s_{N}^{2}(1-(1-b_{0})x^{N}_{0}(t))^{2}\right]\cdot h\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t)\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0}(t))\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)-(1-b_{0})x^{N}_{0}(t)\right)}{h\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t)\right)},

where, for any
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K}
,

h(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(1-x_{0}\right)+b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1}(t))x_{i}.

(2.50)

Moreover, for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[x^{N}_{i}\left(t+\tfrac{1}{N}\right)-x^{N}_{i}(t)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\Bigg]=x^{N}_{i-1}(t)-x^{N}_{i}(t),

(2.51)

and for
i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[{\upsilon}^{N}_{i}\left(t+\tfrac{1}{N}\right)-{\upsilon}^{N}_{i}(t)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\Bigg]=\mathds{1}_{\{i\geq 1\}}{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t)-{\upsilon}^{N}_{i}(t).

(2.52)

Lemma
2.9

will be proved in
Section
5.3
. As in the
cases of constant and slowly changing population sizes studied so far, we
are going to see a separation of timescales. The stochastic process

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
will fluctuate
around the deterministic flow determined by the ODE that arises as a limit
of (
2.49
), (
2.51
)
and (
2.52
) with
time accelerated by a factor
N
. Just as in the
case of a slowly varying population size we were able to discard the

\mathcal{O}(1/N)
increments of population size in writing down the
deterministic flow,
here (
2.45
),
which tells us that the fluctuation of the seed production over a single
generation is of order
N^{-1/2}
, will imply that in the fast
timescale of the ODE flow the random fluctuation of seed production over
a single
generation will be negligible, although we still must record the
fluctuations that happened over the past
K
generations.
As a result, the (accelerated) ODE will be determined by the second term on
the right-hand side
of (
2.49
), and by the
right-hand sides of (
2.51
)
and (
2.52
).
In other words, with a fast-changing environment, we should consider the flow
field

\boldsymbol{F}^{\textrm{fast}}=(F^{\textrm{fast}}_{0},\cdots,F^{\textrm{fast}}_{K},F^{\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},0},\cdots,F^{\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},(K-1)}):[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}F^{\textrm{fast}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\displaystyle{\frac{\left(1-x_{0}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1}(t))x_{i}-(1-b_{0})x_{0}\right)}{(1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}},\\
F^{\textrm{fast}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=x_{i-1}-x_{i}\quad\forall\,i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket,\\
F^{\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},i}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathds{1}_{\{i\geq 1\}}{\upsilon}_{i-1}-{\upsilon}_{i}\quad\forall\,i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket_{0}.\end{array}\right.

(2.53)

Comparing (
2.53
)
to the flow field
\boldsymbol{F}=(F_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

given by (
2.12
), which defines the ODE
arising from the Wright-Fisher model in a constant environment, we observe
that unlike the case of a slowly varyng population size,
the fast environmental fluctuations impact the flow in a way that cannot
be encoded as a simple time change.
Nevertheless, (
2.53
)
indicates that
\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}
should converge to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
so that we should still be able to apply Katzenberger’s machinery in this
context. Recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the diagonal of the
(K+1)
-dimensional
hypercube, and the attractor manifold associated to the ODE with flow given
by
\boldsymbol{F}
. Set

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}:\,\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})=0\Big\}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\times\{0\}^{K}.

(2.54)

Analogous to the definition of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
in (
2.16
), we define

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\textrm{fast}}=({\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{0},\cdots,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{K},0,\cdots,0):[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}

to be the projection map associated with the ODE corresponding to the flow
field
\boldsymbol{F}^{\textrm{fast}}
. In what follows,
\boldsymbol{0}
denotes the
K
-dimensional null vector.

Theorem 2.10
.

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(0),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(0))
converges
to
(x(0),0)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, and that
Assumption
2
holds. Then the
sequence of processes

\left((\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges in distribution in

\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\right)

to a diffusion process with sample paths
in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{fast}}
,
i.e.
(\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to

\boldsymbol{0}
, and
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
a diffusion
(\boldsymbol{x}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
such that

(x_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}

satisfies the stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle x_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left[\frac{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}{2}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})\left(1+2ps^{2}(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\right)+ps^{2}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\right]dt

(2.55)

\displaystyle+2ps^{2}(1-b_{0})\int_{0}^{T}{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}\Bigg[\frac{((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0})}{(B(1-x_{0})+1)}-\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\Bigg]dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sqrt{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}}{(B(1-x_{0})+1)}\,dW_{0}(t),

where
(W_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a standard Brownian motion.

Comparing (
2.55
) with the diffusion limit
of the Wright–Fisher model in a constant
environment (
2.19
), we see that the
noise term in (
2.55
) arises solely from
genetic sampling. In the fast–changing regime the environmental fluctuation
acts uniformly on all individuals of a generation, and in the diffusion
limit this common factor averages out. This differs from classical
fluctuating selection models, in which individuals of different genetic types
are affected differently by a given environment, and the environmental noise
survives in the limit.

Nevertheless, the fast environment influences the limiting SDE through
additional drift terms, since rapid fluctuations alter the forcing flow of
the Wright-Fisher system. Although the flow
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
is too complex to analyse explicitly
for general
K
, our methods allow us to compute the second-order derivatives
of the projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}
that
appear on the right-hand side of (
2.55
) for
every specific
K\in\mathbb{N}
. The expressions become very complex and
so for biological interpretation, we
focus on the case
K=1
.

2.3.1
Biological interpretation for the case
K=1

Recall the formula for
\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}

stated in (
2.21
) for
K=1
. By using Parsons and Rogers
representation of the second order derivatives of the projection map introduced
in
[
46
]
, and our formula for the quadratic approximation
of the non-linear stable manifold
(see Theorem
3.2
),
in Section
6.5

we will establish the
following result.

Lemma 2.11
.

Under the assumption that
K=1
, the following formulae hold for
any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-b_{0})^{2}(1-x_{0})+(1-b_{0})(1-2x)}{((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+1)^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-b_{0})^{2}(1-x_{0})^{2}\Big[(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}^{2}-(1-b_{0})(4-b_{0})x_{0}+(2-b_{0})\Big]}{((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+1)^{3}((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+2)},

and

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\left[(1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})^{2}+(1-2x_{0})\right]}{((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+1)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}(1-x_{0})^{2}\Big[(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}^{2}-(1-b_{0})(5-b_{0})x_{0}+(4-b_{0})\Big]}{((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+1)^{3}((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+2)}.

Still under the assumption
K=1
, we
examine how the parameter
b_{0}
influences
the additional drift terms that appear in the limiting SDE in the
fast–changing environment (see (
2.55
)) but
are absent from the corresponding SDE in the constant–environment
case (
2.19
). To this end, we define
the map
h:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by, for any
(x_{0},b_{0})\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle h(x_{0},b_{0})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(1-b_{0})^{2}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{1}{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})-2(1-b_{0})\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})

(2.56)

\displaystyle+\frac{2(1-b_{0})((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0})}{((1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})+1)}.

Comparing (
2.55
)
with (
2.19
), we see that the
fast-changing environment contributes an additional drift term equal to

\int_{0}^{T}ps^{2}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,h(x_{0},b_{0})\,dt.

(2.57)

We plot the values of the function
h
for different values of

(x_{0},b_{0})\in[0,1]^{2}
in
Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
Contour plot
of the function
h(x_{0},b_{0})
of (
2.56
)
on the domain
[0,1]^{2}
. Darker colours are
associated with larger values of
h
, while lighter colours are associated
with smaller values. For
K=1
,
h
is strictly non-negative,
its maximum value is
4/3
and is achieved at
(0,0)
. The plot suggests
that in the fast-changing environment, the seed bank trait is favoured,
as expected.

Observe that the map
h
is non-negative on its entire domain,
suggesting that the fast-changing environment favours the seed bank trait.
Biologically, this reflects the buffering effect of dormancy against short
environmental events. Moreover, since we may take the values of
s
to be
arbitrarily large in (
2.57
),
the resulting contribution to the drift can exert a substantial influence on
the dynamics.

We also note from Figure
5
that the maximum value of
h
is attained at
(0,0)
, where it equals
4/3
. Biologically, this corresponds to a situation in which the density of mature mutants is extremely low and all mutant seeds skip one generation before maturing. This suggests that the seed bank trait may be particularly effective in protecting a small mutant population in the presence of fast environmental fluctuations.

A limitation of this analysis is that we do not incorporate the impact of the
fast fluctuations on the branching phase. The
analysis of multi-type branching processes in random environments is complex,
and it can rarely be done rigorously for models such as ours
with multiple parameters (see
[
2
, Section 4]
).
Nonetheless, biologically, it could be the case that a constant environment
becomes a fast-changing one after some perturbation. In this case, if the
seed bank trait survives the branching phase and enters the diffusion phase,
the fluctuations can confer an evolutionary advantage to the dormancy trait.

3
Dimension reduction for stochastic differential equations
forced onto a manifold by large drift

In this section, we shall explain, review, and extend some results regarding
the weak convergence of interacting particle systems to SDEs which are forced
onto a manifold by a large drift, enabling a dimension reduction of the
original problem.
In Section
3.1
, we explain
Katzenberger’s results concerning the convergence of stochastic dynamical
systems in the presence of a large drift
[
36
]
.
We will then present,
in Section
3.2
,
a method due to Parsons and Rogers
[
46
]
for computing
the derivatives of the projection map associated to an ODE whose attractor
manifold is one-dimensional. In
Section
3.3
, we apply the
Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction method to develop a general formula for one of
the parameters needed for the Parsons and Rogers method. We illustrate in
Section
3.4
how to apply this formula in
the case of the ODE with flow field given
by (
2.12
) arising from the analysis of
the Wright-Fisher model in constant environment.

3.1
Overview of Katzenberger results

In
[
36
]
, Katzenberger considered a sequence

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of càdlàg

\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
-valued stochastic processes of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(T)=\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}U^{N}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t-))\,d\mathbf{z}^{N}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t-))\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor+{\epsilon}^{N},

(3.1)

where
(\mathbf{z}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of well-behaved
càdlàg
\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
-semimartingales,
(U^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is
a sequence of continuous
(K+1)\times(K+1)
-matrix valued functions,

\mathbf{F}
is a vector field whose associated ODE has an asymptotic
attractor manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
of dimension strictly less than
K+1
,
and
{\epsilon}^{N}
captures the jumps associated with the discrete
approximation of the underlying dynamics. More generally, the integral with
integrand
\mathbf{F}
can be taken with respect to any finite variation
process which asymptotically places infinite mass on compact time intervals.

Assume that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
has sample
paths in some connected subset
\mathscr{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
such
that
\mathscr{U}
is contained in the basin of attraction of the manifold

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\subset\mathscr{U}
. In this case, since we are accelerating the
timescale of the integral of the vector field
\mathbf{F}
, as

N\rightarrow\infty
, if the integrals of
U^{N}
behave sufficiently well,
the distance between the sample paths of
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
and
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}

should converge to
0
. If
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:\mathscr{U}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}

is the projection map associated to the ODE with flow field given by

\mathbf{F}
, then Itô’s formula yields

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(T))=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(0))+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}U^{N}\,d\mathbf{z}^{N}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{hijl}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}U^{N}_{ih}U^{N}_{jl}\,d[z^{N}_{h},z^{N}_{l}](t)+\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{N},

(3.2)

where
\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
denotes the Jacobian matrix of

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
and
\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{N}
captures the corrections due to
jumps of the process. Here, we used the fact that by the definition of the
projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
, we have

\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})\equiv 0
for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{U}
,
c.f. (
2.17
).
If the sequence of semimartingales is sufficiently regular and the sample
paths of
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
converge to a process in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, then

\boldsymbol{x}^{N}-\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})
must converge
to zero, so using (
3.2
)
in the limit we must obtain

\boldsymbol{x}(T)=\boldsymbol{x}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}U\,d\mathbf{z}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{hijl}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}U_{ih}U_{jl}\,d[z_{h},z_{l}](t),

(3.3)

provided
\mathbf{z}^{N}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathbf{z}
,
U^{N}\rightarrow U
, and
{\epsilon}^{N}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}0

as
N\rightarrow\infty
.
Observe that the limiting SDE has sample paths in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
.

Not surprisingly, the proof of convergence requires some regularity
conditions to be satisfied by the sequence

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. For ease of reference, we recall
these conditions and the results that we are going to use
from
[
36
]
.
First we introduce some notation. Define the
open disc

D(1)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:\;|{\lambda}+1|<1\}
,
and let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\subset\mathscr{U}
be the
d
-dimensional connected
attractor manifold of the ODE associated to the vector field
\mathbf{F}
.

Assumption 3

(Attractor manifold)
.

We assume

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{U}:\;\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=0\right\}

to be a twice-continuously differentiable connected
d
-dimensional
manifold, and
\mathbf{F}
to be twice-continuously differentiable. Moreover,
for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}

has exactly (K+1-d) eigenvalues in
D(1)
.

We now turn to the conditions required of the stochastic process.
We denote the filtration of the process
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
by

\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, and assume that it satisfies the usual
conditions. Since we will be applying Katzenberger’s results to the analysis of
a modified Wright-Fisher model, we can and will improve readability by
making stronger assumptions than required by Katzenberger.
We assume that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the semimartingale

\mathbf{z}^{N}
jumps only when
(\lfloor Nt\rfloor)_{t\geq 0}
jumps,
i.e. only at times in the set
\{\frac{i}{N}:\;i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
We also assume

\mathscr{U}
to be compact (since this will be true in our case).

For a finite
variation process
\boldsymbol{A}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

and
T\geq 0
, let
\mathfrak{T}(\boldsymbol{A}^{N},T)
be the total
variation of
\boldsymbol{A}
on the time interval
[0,T]
.

Assumption 4

(Uniform integrability and tightness)
.

For
N\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{z}^{N}
be an

\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\}
- semimartingale with sample paths in

\mathscr{D}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{R}^{K+1})
. Let

\mathbf{z}^{N}=\mathbf{M}^{N}+\mathbf{A}^{N}
, where
\mathbf{M}^{N}
is
a local martingale and
\mathbf{A}^{N}
a finite variation process. We shall
assume:

(i)

For any
T\geq 0
,

\left\{\left[\mathbf{M}^{N}\right](T)+\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{A}^{N},T)\right\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

is uniformly integrable;

(ii)

For any
T\geq 0
,
{\varepsilon}>0
, we have

\lim_{{\delta}\rightarrow 0^{+}}\,\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\leq T}\,\left(\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{A}^{N},t+{\delta})-\mathfrak{T}(\mathbf{A}^{N},t)\right)>{\varepsilon}\right)=0.

We now state Katzenberger’s theorem, which is a simplified version of Theorem 7.2 in
[
36
]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Katzenberger
[
36
]
)
.

Let the sequence of processes
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be
given by (
3.1
). Under
Assumptions
3

and
4
, the sequence

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\mathbf{z}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact
in the space of càdlàg functions

\mathscr{D}\left([0,+\infty),\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\right)
.
Moreover, any limit point
(\boldsymbol{x},\mathbf{z})
is a continuous
semimartingale such that
\boldsymbol{x}(t)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
for every
t
almost
surely, and
(\boldsymbol{x},\mathbf{z})
satisfies the
SDE (
3.3
).

A major challenge in the practical application of
Theorem
3.1

is the computation of the derivatives of the projection map

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
. When the associated ODE has an explicit solution, or
when it admits some characteristic curve, one can compute the derivatives
directly. However, in practice, the ODEs arising in applications are
usually nonlinear, and generally lack an explicit solution. In the next
section, we explain a formula developed by Parsons and Rogers
in
[
46
]
to overcome this problem.

3.2
Parsons and Rogers approach to the computation of
derivatives of projection map

Parsons and Rogers
[
46
]
observed that, when the
attractor manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is one-dimensional, the computation of the
derivatives can be achieved through the analysis of key features of the
underlying dynamical system, concretely the tangent vector to
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the
perpendicular vector to the flow field, the curvature of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, and the
curvature of the nonlinear stable manifold near
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
. To understand
these ideas, we shall briefly recall some concepts regarding dynamical systems.

Let
\mathscr{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
be a connected and compact set, and consider the ODE

\displaystyle\frac{d\boldsymbol{x}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x}),

(3.4)

where
\mathbf{F}:\mathscr{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
is a smooth map
such that if
\boldsymbol{x}(0)\in\mathscr{U}
, then the flow defined
by (
3.4
) never leaves
\mathscr{U}
,
i.e.
\boldsymbol{x}(0)\in\mathscr{U}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{x}(t)\in\mathscr{U}
for all
t\geq 0
. We define the smooth manifold

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{K})\in\mathscr{U}:\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=0\}.

(3.5)

From now on, we make the following assumptions on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
and
\mathbf{F}
.

Assumption 5

(Geometric properties of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
)
.

Equation (
3.5
) defines a
one-dimensional manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, and both
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
and the vector field

\mathbf{F}
satisfy Assumption
3
.
Moreover,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is a curve parametrised by the first coordinate of the
system, i.e. there exists a connected subset

\mathcal{I}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that

\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\Rightarrow x_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}}
, and
a twice-continuously differentiable function

\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}:\mathcal{I}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
such that

\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\Leftrightarrow\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}(x_{0})=\boldsymbol{x}.

As observed in
[
46
, Subsection 2.1]
, the
one-dimensional parametrisation can be relaxed to a local parametrisation,
so that Assumption
5
is not restrictive.
By classical results of dynamical systems theory
[
20
]
,
Assumption
3
implies that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is an
attractor manifold for the ODE (
3.4
).
To formalise this idea, for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{U}
, we define
the flow

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot):[0,+\infty)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathscr{U}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto{\phi}(\boldsymbol{x},T)=\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{T}F(\boldsymbol{{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{x},t))\,dt.

Then, for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{U}
, we can define the map

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:\mathscr{U}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{x},T).

By Assumption
3
, the map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}

is well defined. Moreover, since by
Assumption
5
, the manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}

can be parametrised in terms of the first coordinate, in order to obtain the
first and second order derivatives of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, it
will suffice to compute the first and second order derivatives of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}

on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
.

By Assumption
3
, for any fixed

\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
has
exactly one eigenvalue
0
and
K
eigenvalues with negative real part.
We recall the standard nomenclature from the dynamical systems literature.
Denote by
E_{c}\equiv E_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
the linear space spanned by
the right eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated
with the eigenvalue
0
, and
E_{s}\equiv E_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
the linear
space spanned by the right eigenvectors associated with the other eigenvalues
of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
. We call
E_{c}
the

linear centre manifold
, and
E_{s}
the
linear stable manifold
.
For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, observe that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the
non-linear centre manifold around
\boldsymbol{x}
and by the Centre
Manifold Theorem (see
[
31
, Theorem 3.2.1]
),
E_{c}
is tangent
to
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
. Also by the Centre Manifold Theorem,
there exists a unique invariant
K
-dimensional manifold

\mathfrak{W}_{s}\equiv\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
, tangent to

E_{s}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
, such that for all

\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}
, we have

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{y})=\boldsymbol{x}
.

For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,
let
\boldsymbol{v}\equiv\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

be the left eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated
with the eigenvalue
0
,
i.e.
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})=0
, with
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})\neq 0
.
By Assumption
5
,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is a
twice-continuously differentiable curve of the first coordinate, and so
without loss of generality we use the parametrisation

\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\boldsymbol{v}(x_{0})
.
Parsons and Rogers observe (see Equation (12) in
[
46
]
)
that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the set of points

\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbf{R}^{K+1}\cap\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})
such that
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})

is approximated up to second order by

\Big\langle\boldsymbol{v}(x_{0}),\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}(x_{0})\Big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}(x_{0}),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}(x_{0})(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}(x_{0}))\Big\rangle+\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}^{3})=0,

(3.6)

where
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}(x_{0})
is a symmetric matrix describing the
curvature of
\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
.

Remark
.

We emphasise that Equation (
3.6
)
differs from Equation (12) in
[
46
]
by the
factor
\frac{1}{2}
in front of the second term on the left-hand side.
As a result, the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}(x_{0})

in (
3.6
) is exactly 2 times
the corresponding matrix
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}
in
[
46
]
. Although this results in a minor
inconvenience when directly comparing the two papers, the normalisatation
in (
3.6
) will be convenient for
our quadratic approximation of the nonlinear stable manifold

\mathfrak{W}_{s}
in Section
3.3
.

By taking a Taylor expansion up to second order of the left-hand side
of (
3.6
), Parsons and Rogers
are able to derive explicit expressions for the derivatives of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}
.
For the first order derivatives, for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{v_{i}}{\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}}\quad\forall i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0},

(3.7)

and for the second order derivatives,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}}\left(v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}+v_{j}^{\prime}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}-{\theta}_{ij}-\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}\sum_{l=0}^{K}(2v_{l}^{\prime}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}+v_{l}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime\prime})\right)\quad\forall i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}.

(3.8)

Observe that in
both (
3.7
)
and (
3.8
),
the derivatives of the coordinates of
\boldsymbol{v}
and

\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}
are taken with respect to
x_{0}
. Hence,
Parsons and Rogers reduce the problem of computing the first and second order
derivatives of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}
to a problem of characterising the dynamical system
near the attractor manifold. Moreover, neither the parametrisation of

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
given by
\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}
nor the left eigenvector

\boldsymbol{v}
should be difficult to compute, since they follow from
standard computations concerning the Jacobian of the vector field
\mathbf{F}
.

The challenge is then to compute the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
.
In
[
46
, Appendix B]
, Parsons and Rogers propose
performing this
computation by finding an explicit basis of generalised eigenvectors of

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
and changing the coordinates near
a point
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
. Although this is the usual approach
in the literature on dynamical systems, it is not feasible for our
application for large values of
K
, since for
K\geq 5
, the characteristic
polynomial of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
may not have explicit
roots. In the next section, we find an alternative formula for

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
which sidesteps the explicit computation of
the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

3.3
Lyapunov-Schmidt method for the approximation of the non-linear stable manifold

In this section, we present an approach to computing the curvature of
the nonlinear stable manifold that does not rely on the computation of a
basis of generalised eigenvectors of the Jacobian matrix of the flow.
We emphasise that although our primary aim is to apply this approach to
the ODE arising from our model, the results of this section are applicable
to any dynamical system satisfying
Assumptions
3

and
5
.

We continue to work in the setting of
Section
3.2
,
i.e. assume we are studying the ODE (
3.4
)
which has an invariant attractor manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
defined
by (
3.5
), and
Assumptions
3

and
5
are both satisfied.
For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, let

\boldsymbol{u}\equiv\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
be a right eigenvector
of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated with the eigenvalue
0
,
and let
\boldsymbol{v}\equiv\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})
be a left
eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated with the
eigenvalue
0
. We choose the pair of vectors
\boldsymbol{u}
and

\boldsymbol{v}
so that
\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=1
.
We assume that both
\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
are known.
Observe that for each
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the linear centre manifold

E_{c}\equiv E_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
is given by

E_{c}=\spanvector(\boldsymbol{u})
.
Let
E_{s}\equiv E_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
be the linear stable manifold spanned
by the right eigenvectors of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}

associated with
the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
with
negative real part. Recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is a non-linear centre manifold, and
let
\mathfrak{W}_{s}\equiv\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
be the
non-linear stable manifold tangent to
E_{s}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
.

We will apply the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction method to approximate

\mathfrak{W}_{s}
up to second order. See for instance Section I.3 and
Chapter VII in
[
29
]
, or
Chapter 5 in
[
40
]
for applications
of this method to the study of bifurcations.

Define the linear maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{c}:\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow E_{c}

(3.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y})=\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{u},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow E_{s}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{s}(\boldsymbol{y})=\boldsymbol{y}-\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{u}.

Note that we can also write
\mathcal{P}_{s}=\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P}_{c}
,
where
\mathbf{I}:\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
is the
identity map. Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{c}
is the map which projects

\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
onto the linear centre manifold
E_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
,
while
\mathcal{P}_{s}
projects
\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
onto the linear stable
manifold
E_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
.

Our next result shows that the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
can be computed
in terms of
\boldsymbol{u}
,
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\mathcal{P}_{s}
, and using
the Jacobian and the Hessians of the flow map at
\boldsymbol{x}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

The matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}

in (
3.6
) is the unique symmetric
square matrix of order
K+1
satisfying the following identities

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.12)

Theorem
3.2
,
combined with Katzenberger’s result
(Theorem
3.1
) and
the formulae (
3.7
)
and (
3.8
) of
Parsons and Rogers, allows us to explicitly characterise the limiting
diffusion process for finite-dimensional stochastic dynamical systems of
arbitrarily large order, provided that
Assumptions
3
,
4

and
5
are satisfied.
The proof of
Theorem
3.2

relies on an application of the Centre Manifold Theorem. We
postpone it until Section
6.2
.

Note that Equation (
3.11
) is a Lyapunov equation
(also known as a Silvester equation), of semi-stable type. The term

semi-stable
refers to the fact that the Jacobian matrix

\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{F_{x}}
has one eigenvalue
0
and
K
eigenvalues with
negative real part.
Stable Lyapunov equations are matrix equations of the form

\boldsymbol{A}^{*}\boldsymbol{Y}+\boldsymbol{Y}\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{C},

in which
\boldsymbol{A}
,
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}
are square
matrices of order
K+1
, all the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{A}
have
negative real part, and
\boldsymbol{C}
is symmetric. Stable Lyapunov
equations are often used in control theory to study the asymptotic behaviour
of linear systems (see for instance
[
50
, Chapter 5]
).

Although from an abstract perspective,
Theorem
3.2

completely characterises the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
, the linear
Lyapunov equation in (
3.11
) can be
computationally expensive. In our setting, when
K
is large, solving
Equation (
3.11
)
analytically is not feasible. Instead, in order to understand the
behaviour of the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
,
we shall adapt a strategy
borrowed from the theory of stable Lyapunov equations,
and directly classify the solution matrix as positive or negative
(semi)definite .

Recall that a real symmetric matrix
\mathbf{M}
of order
K+1
is called
positive definite (resp. positive semidefinite) if and only if

\langle\boldsymbol{y},\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle>0
for all

\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\setminus\{0\}
,
(resp. if and only if

\langle\boldsymbol{y},\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle\geq 0

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
).
Similarly,
\mathbf{M}
is negative definite (resp. negative semidefinite)
if and only if
\langle\boldsymbol{y},\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle<0

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\setminus\{0\}

(resp. if and only if

\langle\boldsymbol{y},\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle\leq 0

for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
).
A real symmetric matrix is positive semidefinite (resp. negative semidefinite)
if and only if all its eigenvalues are nonnegative (resp. not positive),
see e.g.
[
10
, Chapter 1]
for a review.
We define a partial order
\succeq
on the space of real symmetric
matrices by
\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{Q}
if and only if

\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{Q}
is positive semidefinite.
We also write
\mathbf{M}\succeq 0
(resp.
\preceq 0
) if and only if

\mathbf{M}
is positive semidefinite (resp. negative semidefinite).
We are ready to state a formula for the solution of
Equation (
3.11
) that will give us information
about the signs of the real part of the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
be the unique symmetric matrix satisfying
both (
3.11
)
and (
3.12
).
Then,
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
satisfies the formula

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\,dt.

Moreover, if

\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\succeq 0

(resp.
\preceq 0
), then
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\preceq 0
(resp.
\succeq 0
).

The proof of Theorem
3.3

is also postponed until Section
6.2
.
We observe that the presence of the projection matrices

\mathcal{P}_{s}
and
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}
inside the integral formula is
pivotal for the proof of
Theorem
3.3
. Indeed,
if we do not include these matrices, the integral is not well defined.
In particular, this construction with projection matrices could also be
applied to other semistable Lyapunov equations. This would correct a small
mistake made by Parsons and Rogers in Equation (C.9)
in
[
46
]
, in which they represent the solution of a
semi-stable Lyapunov equation in integral form without explicitly inserting
the projection matrices inside the integrand.

3.4
Application to a specific ODE

In this section, we will apply
Theorems
3.2

and
3.3
to study
the particular ODE arising from our Wright-Fisher model with seed bank.

Recall the flow field
\mathbf{F}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

given by (
2.12
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}F_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\displaystyle{\frac{(1-x_{0})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}-(1-b_{0})x_{0}\right)}{(1-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}},\\
F_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=x_{i-1}-x_{i}\quad\forall\,i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket.\end{array}\right.

As explained in Section
2
, for each

i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
,
b_{i}
represents the probability of a
seed from a mutant individual being ready to germinate in exactly
i

generations. We assume that the vector

\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}

satisfies (
2.1
).
Recall also that the non-linear centre manifold

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{K+1}:\,\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=0\}
in this example
is the diagonal of the
(K+1)
-dimensional
hypercube:

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\{\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{K+1}:\,x_{0}=x_{1}=\ldots=x_{K}\Big\}.

The parametrisation of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is then trivially
given by the map
\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, where,
for all
x\in[0,1]
,

\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
x\\
x\\
\vdots\\
x\end{array}\right).

(3.13)

We wish to study the behaviour of
\mathbf{F}
near
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
.
For any
\boldsymbol{x}=(x,\ldots,x)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,
the Jacobian of
\mathbf{F}
evaluated at
\boldsymbol{x}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&b_{1}(1-x)&b_{2}(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\\
1&-1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&-1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&-1\end{array}\right).

(3.14)

We first check that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
satisfies
Assumption
3
with respect to the
map
\mathbf{F}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

For every
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the Jacobian matrix of the flow

\mathbf{F}
evaluated at
\boldsymbol{x}
has exactly one eigenvalue equal
to
0
and
K
eigenvalues in the open disk

D(1)=\{{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:\;|{\lambda}+1|<1\}
.

Proof.

Observe that
{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
if and only
{\lambda}+1
is an
eigenvalue of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}
,
where
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}
is the
square matrix of order
K+1
given by

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}-(1-b_{0})(1-x)+1&b_{1}(1-x)&b_{2}(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\\
1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{array}\right).

By directly computing the characteristic polynomial
\tilde{p}
of

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}
, we obtain that
for all
\tilde{{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\tilde{p}(\tilde{{\lambda}})=(-1)^{K}\tilde{{\lambda}}^{K}({-(1-b_{0})(1-x)}+1-\tilde{{\lambda}})+(-1)^{K}{(1-x)}\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}\tilde{{\lambda}}^{K-i}.

(3.15)

To prove our claim, it suffices to establish that
\tilde{p}
has exactly
K

roots satisfying
|\tilde{{\lambda}}|<1
. To see that this holds,
note that since
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}

is a strictly positive matrix, by the Perron-Frobenius Theorem,

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}
has a simple
eigenvalue

\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}
which is a real positive number,
and which is strictly greater than the modulus of all other
eigenvalues.

We claim that

\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}=1
. Indeed, since

(1-b_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}
,
\tilde{{\lambda}}=1
is a root
of
\tilde{p}
. Suppose now that

\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}>1
.
In this case, by dividing the characteristic polynomial
\tilde{p}
by

(\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)})^{K}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={-(1-b_{0})(1-x)}+1-\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}+{(1-x)}\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}^{-i}

\displaystyle<{-(1-b_{0})(1-x)}+1-\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}+{(1-b_{0})(1-x)}

\displaystyle<0,

where we used that
1-b_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}
to derive the second
inequality, and the assumption

\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}>1
to obtain the final one.
Since this is a contradiction, we conclude that

\tilde{{\lambda}}_{\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}\right)}=1
, and the result follows.
∎

Lemma
3.4
implies that
the diagonal
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is an attractor manifold, and therefore we can define
the projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
as
in (
2.16
). We would like to apply
the formulae (
3.7
)
and (
3.8
) of
Parsons and Rogers to compute the first and second order derivatives of

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}
. With this in mind, let

\boldsymbol{u}\equiv\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}

be a right eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated
with the eigenvalue
0
. To simplify computations, we set

\boldsymbol{u}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
1\\
1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{array}\right).

(3.16)

We want to take
\boldsymbol{v}\equiv\boldsymbol{v}({\boldsymbol{x}})

to be the left
eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated with the
eigenvalue
0

(i.e.
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}=0
)
for which
\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle=1
.
This gives

\boldsymbol{v}=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
(1-b_{0})(1-x)\\
\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)\\
\vdots\\
b_{K}(1-x)\end{array}\right),

(3.17)

where as usual
B=\sum_{i=1}^{K}ib_{i}
represents the mean germination time.
We are now in a position to use
Equation (
3.7
)
to compute the first order derivates of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}
and
prove identity (
2.20
).

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i=0}^{K}
satisfying
Assumption
2.1
, and
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, we have

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{(B(1-x_{0})+1)}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{\left(\sum_{j=i}^{K}b_{j}\right)(1-x_{0})}{(B(1-x_{0})+1)}\;\quad\forall i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket.

Proof.

From (
3.13
), the
parametrisation curve
\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}
satisfies

{\gamma}^{\prime}(x_{0})=\boldsymbol{1}=(1,1,1,\ldots,1)^{*}\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=0,

so that

\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}{\gamma}^{\prime}_{l}\equiv 1.

Substituting in (
3.7
)
(using (
3.17
)) completes the proof.
∎

We now turn to properties of the second order derivatives.
Since our aim is to derive these properties for general
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
including in particular
K>5
, we
apply the Lyapunov-Schmidt method described in the previous section.
We start by observing that the linear space spanned by
\boldsymbol{u}
is the
linear centre manifold
E_{c}\equiv E_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
at

\boldsymbol{x}
. By (
3.9
), the
projection
\mathcal{P}_{c}
onto
E_{c}
is given by

\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y})=\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{u}
.
It will be convenient to represent
\mathcal{P}_{c}
as a matrix

\mathcal{P}_{c}=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&(1-b_{0})(1-x)&\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\\
1&(1-b_{0})(1-x)&\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\\
1&(1-b_{0})(1-x)&\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&(1-b_{0})(1-x)&\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)\end{array}\right).

(3.18)

To simplify notation, for all
j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, we define

B_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B-\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}.

(3.19)

From identity (
3.10
),
the projection matrix
\mathcal{P}_{s}
onto the linear stable manifold

E_{s}
is given by
\mathcal{P}_{s}=\mathbf{I}_{K+1}-\mathcal{P}_{c}
.
Substituting the
expression (
3.18
),
we find

\displaystyle(B(1-x)+1)\mathcal{P}_{s}

(3.20)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}B(1-x)&-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&-b_{K}(1-x)\\
-1&B_{1}(1-x)+1&-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&-b_{K}(1-x)\\
-1&-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&B_{2}(1-x)+1&\cdots&-b_{K}(1-x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&\cdots&B_{K}(1-x)+1\end{array}\right),

with transpose
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}

\displaystyle(B(1-x)+1)\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}

(3.21)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}B(1-x)&-1&-1&\cdots&-1\\
-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&B_{1}(1-x)+1&-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&\cdots&-(1-b_{0})(1-x)\\
-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)&B_{2}(1-x)+1&\cdots&-\left(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\right)(1-x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-b_{K}(1-x)&-b_{K}(1-x)&-b_{K}(1-x)&\cdots&B_{K}(1-x)+1\end{array}\right).

Let
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
be the matrix describing the curvature of the
non-linear stable manifold
\mathfrak{W}_{s}\equiv\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
. We apply Theorem
3.2
.
In particular,
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is a symmetric square matrix of
order
K+1

satisfying
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
. By the definition of
the right eigenvector
\boldsymbol{u}

in (
3.16
) and the fact that

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is symmetric, this last condition can be written as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\theta}_{00}+{\theta}_{01}+{\theta}_{02}+\cdots+{\theta}_{0K}&=&0\\
{\theta}_{01}+{\theta}_{11}+{\theta}_{12}+\cdots+{\theta}_{1K}&=&0\\
{\theta}_{02}+{\theta}_{12}+{\theta}_{22}+\cdots+{\theta}_{2K}&=&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
{\theta}_{0K}+{\theta}_{1K}+{\theta}_{2K}+\cdots+{\theta}_{KK}&=&0\end{array}\right..

(3.22)

Moreover,
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
satisfies the semistable Lyapunov equation

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}.

(3.23)

We wish then to solve (
3.23
)
and (
3.22
). First we compute the terms that
appear on each side of (
3.23
). Starting
with the left-hand side, note that by (
3.14
),
the transpose of the Jacobian of
\mathbf{F}
is given by

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&1&0&\cdots&0\\
b_{1}(1-x)&-1&1&\cdots&0\\
b_{2}(1-x)&0&-1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{K}(1-x)&0&0&\cdots&-1\end{array}\right).

(3.24)

Combining (
3.14
)
and (
3.24
), we have that for all

i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{00}&=&-2(1-b_{0})(1-x){\theta}_{00}+2{\theta}_{01},\\
\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{0j}&=&b_{j}(1-x){\theta}_{00}+(-(1-b_{0})(1-x)-1){\theta}_{0j}+\mathds{1}_{\{j<K\}}{\theta}_{0,(j+1)}+{\theta}_{1j},\\
\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{ij}&=&b_{i}(1-x){\theta}_{0j}+b_{j}(1-x){\theta}_{0i}-2{\theta}_{ij}+\mathds{1}_{\{i<K\}}{\theta}_{i+1,j}+\mathds{1}_{\{j<K\}}{\theta}_{i,j+1}.\end{array}\right.

(3.25)

We now compute the right-hand side
of (
3.23
).
Observe that from the definition of the
flow (
2.12
), for all

i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
we have

\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\equiv 0
. Therefore, we conclude that

\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}=v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}.

Recalling the definition of
\mathbf{F}

in (
2.12
), we can compute the second order
derivatives of
F_{0}
on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, obtaining for all

\boldsymbol{x}=(x,\ldots,x)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\displaystyle{\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}F_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})}&=&2(1-b_{0})-2(1-b_{0})^{2}(1-x),\\
\displaystyle{\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}F_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})}&=&2b_{j}(1-b_{0})(1-x)-b_{j}\quad\forall j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket,\\
\displaystyle{\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}F_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})}&=&-2b_{i}b_{j}(1-x)\quad\forall i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket.\end{array}\right.

(3.26)

It will be convenient to decompose
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})
as

\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})=\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})+2(1-x){\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}},

(3.27)

where

\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}2(1-b_{0})&-b_{1}&-b_{2}&\cdots&-b_{K}\\
-b_{1}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
-b_{2}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-b_{K}&0&0&\cdots&0\end{array}\right),

(3.28)

and

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}-(1-b_{0})^{2}&b_{1}(1-b_{0})&b_{2}(1-b_{0})&\cdots&b_{K}(1-b_{0})\\
b_{1}(1-b_{0})&-b_{1}^{2}&-b_{1}b_{2}&\cdots&-b_{1}b_{K}\\
b_{2}(1-b_{0})&-b_{1}b_{2}&-b_{2}^{2}&\cdots&-b_{2}b_{K}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{K}(1-b_{0})&-b_{1}b_{K}&-b_{2}b_{K}&\cdots&-b_{K}^{2}\end{array}\right).

(3.29)

Observe from (
3.28
) that

\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})
is the Hessian of the function

G_{0}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given
in (
2.24
).
Since the Lyapunov equation is linear, it is enough to find symmetric matrices

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}
and
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}

solving Equations (
3.22
)
and (
3.23
), where in the last equation
we replace
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})
by
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})

and
2(1-x){\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
, respectively. There are no straightforward techniques for
finding solutions to the systems of linear equations that are valid for any

K\in\mathbb{N}
. One possible strategy is to solve the linear system for
small values of
K
in order to get some intuition for what the solution
should look like, and then prove directly that the candidate solution satisfies
the linear system. If uniqueness holds, as it does in our case, this strategy,
when applicable, allows us to rigorously establish the solution to the system.
This is the strategy that we will follow to compute
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i=0}^{K}

satisfying Equation (
2.1
),
the symmetric matrix

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\theta}^{G}_{ij})_{i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}
is such that for any

i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, the following formulae hold

\displaystyle{\theta}^{G}_{00}=-\frac{B^{2}(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}},\;{\theta}^{G}_{0i}=\frac{B\left(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}\;\textrm{ and }{\theta}^{G}_{ij}=-\frac{\left(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\right)\left(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}.

Proof.

We proceed directly by plugging the given expressions into
Equations (
3.22
)
and (
3.23
). First, since we can write

B=\sum_{i=1}^{K}ib_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l},

it follows immediately thta

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\boldsymbol{u}=0
.
We must check that it also satisfies (
3.23
). Substituting into (
3.25
), we conclude
that for all
i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{2B(1-b_{0})(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}},

(3.30)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{0j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(Bb_{j}+(1-b_{0})\left(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\right)\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(b_{i}\left(\sum_{l=j}^{K}b_{l}\right)+b_{j}\left(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\right)\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}.

To conclude the proof, we compute the terms
of
v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}
.
We start by computing
v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}
.
Observe that
combining (
3.17
), (
3.20
), (
3.28
)
and the fact that
\sum_{j=1}^{K}b_{j}=1-b_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}\left(B(1-x)\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})\right)

(3.31)

\displaystyle=\frac{2B(1-b_{0})(1-x)+(1-b_{0})}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-b_{0})(2B(1-x)+1)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}.

Moreover, for all
j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{0j}

(3.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}\Biggl[-\Biggl(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\Biggl)(1-x)\Biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=0,\\
l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{K}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{l}}\Biggl)+(B_{j}(1-x)+1)\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}\Biggl]

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{(1-b_{0})\left(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\right)(1-x)+(B(1-x)+1)b_{j}}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}\right),

where for the second equality we used that,
by (
3.19
),
B_{j}=B-\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{s}
given
in (
3.20
) and
the computation of the Hessian (
3.28
),
we also have for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{i0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}\Biggl(B(1-x)\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}\Biggl)

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\frac{-Bb_{i}(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}.

Returning to the computation of

v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}
, for all

i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, we have, since

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}\equiv 0
for

i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{ij}

\displaystyle=v_{0}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x})(\mathcal{P}_{s})_{0j}+v_{0}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})(\mathcal{P}_{s})_{jj}+v_{0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1,\\
l\neq j\end{subarray}}^{K}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}G_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{l}}(\mathcal{P}_{s})_{lj}

(3.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{i}\Big(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\Big)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}.

We now compute

v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}
.
Using the expression (
3.21
)
for
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}

and the identities (
3.31
), (
3.33
)
and (
3.30
), we have

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{B(1-x)\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{00}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{i0}}{(B(1-x)+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2B(1-b_{0})(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{00},

as desired.
Moreover by identities (
3.21
), (
3.32
), (
3.34
) and (
3.30
),
we conclude that for any
j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{0j}

\displaystyle=\frac{B(1-x)\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{0j}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{ij}}{(B(1-x)+1)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(Bb_{j}+(1-b_{0})\left(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\right)\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{0j}.

It remains then to compute the terms that do not belong to the first row
of the matrix.
By identities (
3.30
), (
3.21
), (
3.32
), and (
3.34
),
we have for
i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\Big(v_{0}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Big)_{i0}\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{0j}+\Big(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Big)_{ii}\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{ij}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=1,\\
r\neq i\end{subarray}}^{K}\Big(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Big)_{ir}\Big(v_{0}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s}\Big)_{rj}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Big(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\Big)\Big(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\Big)(1-b_{0})(1-x)^{2}+b_{j}\Big(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\Big)(1-x)(B(1-x)+1)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{b_{i}\Big(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\Big)(1-x)(B_{i}(1-x)+1)-\Big(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\Big)\Big(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\Big)\Big(\sum_{r=1,\,r\neq i}^{K}b_{r}\Big)(1-x)^{2}}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(b_{i}\left(\sum_{l=j}^{K}b_{l}\right)+b_{j}\left(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\right)\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)_{ij},

where we used
B_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B-\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}
and

\sum_{r=1,\,r\neq i}^{K}b_{r}=1-b_{0}-b_{i}
in the third equality.
Since all the terms agree, we conclude
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{G}
is indeed the
desired matrix.
∎

It remains to analyse
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}
. Unfortunately, here we
have not been able to derive a general formula which is valid for any

K\in\mathbb{N}
. Clearly, if one has some specific value of
K
in mind,
then our approach allows the computation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}

for that particular case. We would like however to derive properties of the
system that will hold for at least a large set of possible values of
K
.
We will then use
Theorem
3.3
to obtain
some qualitative behaviour of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
. To follow this strategy,
we observe that by (
3.29
),

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is a real symmetric matrix such that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
.
This observation simplifies our analysis.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
be the square matrix given
by (
3.29
). Then, for any
fixed
K\in\mathbb{N}
, the characteristic polynomial of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is
given by

p_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}({\lambda})=(-1)^{K+1}{\lambda}^{K}\left({\lambda}+(1-b_{0})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}^{2}\right).

In particular,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is negative semidefinite.

Proof.

It is convenient to express the characteristic polynomial in terms of
the principal minors of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
. Recall that a submatrix

\mathbf{M}
of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is called a principal submatrix of order
n
if
there exist
0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{n}\leq K
such that

\mathbf{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{1}i_{1}}&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}&\cdots&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{1}i_{n}}\\
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{2}i_{2}}&\cdots&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{2}i_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{1}i_{n}}&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{2}i_{n}}&\cdots&{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}_{i_{n}i_{n}}\end{array}\right).

Denoting by
c_{i}
the sum of the determinants of the principal submatrices
of order
i
of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
, we can express the characteristic polynomial of

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
as (see for instance
[
17
, Theorem 4.3.1]
)

p_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}({\lambda})=(-1)^{K+1}{\lambda}^{K+1}+\sum_{i=0}^{K}(-1)^{i}{\lambda}^{i}c_{K+1-i}.

(3.35)

To prove our claim, it will be enough to compute the sum of the minors.
For
i=K
, we have

c_{1}=\tr({\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}})=-(1-b_{0})^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}^{2}
.
We now claim that
c_{i}=0
for
i>1
. In fact, we shall verify that
