the determinant of any principal submatrix of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
with order higher
than
1
is zero. Indeed, writing
{\delta}_{0}=(1-b_{0})
and

{\delta}_{i}=-b_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, by the
definition of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}

in (
3.29
),
the determinant of any principal minor
\mathbf{M}
of order
n
of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}

can be written as

\det(\mathbf{M})=(-1)^{n}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccc}{\delta}_{i_{1}}{\delta}_{i_{1}}&{\delta}_{i_{1}}{\delta}_{i_{2}}&\cdots&{\delta}_{i_{1}}{\delta}_{i_{n}}\\
{\delta}_{i_{1}}{\delta}_{i_{2}}&{\delta}_{i_{2}}{\delta}_{i_{2}}&\cdots&{\delta}_{i_{2}}{\delta}_{i_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\delta}_{i_{1}}{\delta}_{i_{n}}&{\delta}_{i_{2}}{\delta}_{i_{n}}&\cdots&{\delta}_{i_{n}}{\delta}_{i_{n}}\end{array}\right|=0,

since the rows are linearly dependent. Our claim now follows
from (
3.35
).
This implies that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
has the eigenvalue
0
with algebraic
multiplicity
K
, and one negative eigenvalue equal to

-\left((1-b_{0})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}^{2}\right)
.
In particular,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is negative semidefinite, which completes the proof.
∎

We are now in position to prove
Proposition
2.4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

By the
formula (
3.8
) of
Parsons and Rogers, for every
i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}}\left(v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}+v_{j}^{\prime}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}-{\theta}^{G}_{ij}-{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{ij}\right).

From the expression for the left eigenvector
v

in (
3.17
), and the fact that the
diagonal
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
has zero curvature, we have

\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}=1
,

\sum_{l=0}^{K}v_{l}^{\prime}{\gamma}_{l}^{\prime}=0
and
\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}^{\prime\prime}=0
.
Differentiating
\boldsymbol{v}
with respect to
x_{0}
, recalling the formula for the first order derivative
in Lemma
3.5
, and
applying Lemma
3.6
,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{B(B(1-x)+2)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}-{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{00},

(3.36)

while for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x})=-\frac{\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}-{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{0i},

(3.37)

and finally for
i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x})=-\frac{\left(\sum_{l=i}^{K}b_{l}\right)\left(\sum_{q=j}^{K}b_{q}\right)(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}-{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{ij}.

(3.38)

Now, by Lemma
3.7
,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}
is negative semidefinite, and therefore Proposition
3.3
implies
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}
is positive semidefinite. In particular, the diagonal terms of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}
are nonnegative, and therefore by (
3.36
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})\leq\frac{B(B(1-x)+2)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}\;\forall\,x\in[0,1].

Moreover, when
x=1
, the decomposition of
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})

in (
3.27
) yields

\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})=\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(G_{0})
.
Therefore,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{1})=2B.

It remains then to verify the monotonicity property. To see this, observe
that for
x\in[0,1]
,

\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{B(B(1-x)+2)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{3}}\right)=\frac{B^{2}(2B(1-x)+5)}{(B(1-x)+1)^{4}}>0,

whenever
b_{0}<1
(see (
2.3
)). Moreover, since the function

x\mapsto\frac{(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)}
is decreasing in
[0,1]
, by
Lemma
3.7
, we have for any

0\leq x\leq\tilde{x}\leq 1
,

\frac{(1-x)}{(B(1-x)+1)}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}\preceq\frac{(1-\tilde{x})}{(B(1-\tilde{x})+1)}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}},

which implies (by
Proposition
3.3
)
that the function
x\mapsto{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{00}(x)
is monotonically
non-increasing on
[0,1]
.
From (
3.36
), we conclude
that the function

x\mapsto\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(x,x,\ldots,x)

is strictly increasing on
[0,1]
as desired, and the proof is complete.
∎

4
Analysis of the Branching Phase

In this section, we provide the (rather standard)
proof of Theorem
2.1
,
concerning the multitype branching process approximation to the
Wright-Fisher model in a constant environment, valid while the mature mutant
plants constitute a negligible proportion of the population.
Recall from
Section
2.1

that
(Z_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
represents the number of
mature mutant individuals in the population and that the mean
matrix
\mathbf{M}

of the process
\mathbf{Z}
(which also records numbers and time until
germination of seeds in the seed bank) is given
by (
2.5
).

Since all entries of
\mathbf{M}
are nonnegative, by the
Perron-Frobenius Theorem,
\mathbf{M}
has a maximal eigenvalue

{\lambda}_{\mathbf{M}}
which is strictly positive, and such that all the
other eigenvalues have modulus strictly less then
{\lambda}_{\mathbf{M}}
.
To confirm that the branching process
\mathbf{Z}
is critical, we compute
the value of
{\lambda}_{\mathbf{M}}

(see
[
3
, Section V.7]
).

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
K\in\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{i})_{i=0}^{K}
, the
Perron-Frobenius eigenvalue
{\lambda}_{\mathbf{M}}
of
\mathbf{M}
is
equal to
1
, i.e. the multitype branching process
\mathbf{Z}
is critical.

Proof.

It suffices to note that the matrix
\mathbf{M}
has the same
characteristic polynomial as the matrix

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{0}}+\mathbf{I}_{K+1}
that appears in
the proof of Lemma
3.4
,
for which we already proved that
{\lambda}_{\mathbf{M}}=1
, as desired.
∎

Since
\mathbf{Z}
is a critical multitype branching process, we can
apply the results of Mullikin
[
44
]
to understand its
asymptotic behaviour. For this, we must find the right and left eigenvectors

\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
of the mean matrix
\mathbf{M}
,
associated to the eigenvalue
1
, and normalised so that

\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{1}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=1
.
By (
2.5
),

\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{(M+K)}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}M\\
1\\
1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{array}\right)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{v}=\frac{(M+K)}{M(B+1)}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
M(1-b_{0})\\
M\Big(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}\Big)\\
\vdots\\
Mb_{K}\end{array}\right).

(4.1)

In particular,

u_{0}v_{0}=\frac{1}{(B+1)},

(4.2)

where
B
is the mean germination time of the seed bank.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Let
Z^{(i)}_{j}
be the number of particles of type
j
generated by the
branching of a particle of type
i
. For any

l\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
, we let

q^{(l)}(i,j)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{(l)}_{i}Z^{(l)}_{j}-{\delta}_{ij}Z^{(l)}_{i}\right],

where
{\delta}_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta.
By direct computation and recalling that each type
0
particle generates

\Poiss(b_{0})
offspring of type
0
and
\Poiss(Mb_{i})
offspring of
type
i
, for
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, and that each non-mature
seed gives birth to at most one offspring during a ‘branching’ event,
we conclude that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccll}q^{(0)}(0,0)&=&b_{0}^{2},&\\
q^{(0)}(0,j)&=&Mb_{0}b_{j}&\forall\,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket,\\
q^{(0)}(i,j)&=&M^{2}b_{i}b_{j}&\forall\,i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket,\\
q^{(l)}(i,j)&=&0&\forall\,l\in\llbracket K\rrbracket\textrm{ and }\forall\,i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}.\end{array}\right.

For
l\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
, and

\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, we define the quadratic form

Q^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{w})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=0}^{K}w_{i}q^{(l)}(i,j)w_{j}.

For any
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, let

\mathbf{Q}(\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
be given by

\mathbf{Q}(\boldsymbol{w})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(Q^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{w}),\cdots,Q^{(K)}(\boldsymbol{w}))
. Then, by explicitly computing

\mathbf{Q}
, we conclude that for

\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, we have

Q^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{w})=\frac{M^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\,\sum_{j=1}b_{i}b_{j}w_{i}w_{j}+Mb_{0}w_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}b_{j}w_{j}+\frac{b_{0}^{2}w_{0}^{2}}{2},

and
Q^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{w})=0
for
l\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
.
For the particular case
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}
, where

\boldsymbol{u}
is the right Frobenius eigenvector of
\mathbf{M}
, we
conclude from (
4.1
) that

\displaystyle Q^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{u})=\frac{u_{0}^{2}}{2}.

(4.3)

Let
\mathbf{e}_{i}
be the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
which has
coordinate
1
in position
i
and
0
otherwise. Then, using the
results of Mullikin
[
44
]
,
if the branching process
\mathbf{Z}
starts from one mature mutant plant,
i.e. if
Z_{0}=\mathbf{e}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{e}_{0}}\left(\mathbf{Z}(t)\neq 0\right)=\frac{\langle\mathbf{e}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle}{\langle\boldsymbol{v},\mathbf{Q}(\boldsymbol{u})\rangle}=\frac{u_{0}}{v_{0}Q^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{u})}=\frac{2}{u_{0}v_{0}}=2(B+1),

where, as usual,
B
is the mean germination time of the seed bank, and
we substituted the expressions (
4.3
)
and (
4.2
). Moreover,
using
[
44
, Theorem 9]
, for any
y\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{e}_{0}}\left(\frac{Z_{0}(t)}{t}\geq y\,\Big|\,\mathbf{Z}(t)\neq 0\right)

\displaystyle=1-\exp\left(\frac{y}{\langle\mathbf{e}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle\cdot\langle\mathbf{Q}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\exp\left(-2y(B+1)^{2}\right),

where we used Equations (
4.3
)
and (
4.2
) to obtain the last equality.
Our claim then holds.
∎

We now proceed to the proof of
Lemma
2.2
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Since
{\psi}_{B}(0)=0
,
by (
2.8
),
in order to compute an explicit formula for
{\psi}_{B}(y)
, it is enough to
observe that for any
y\in[0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle(B+1)(1-\exp(-2{\psi}_{B}(y)(B+1)^{2}))=1-\exp(-2y),

which implies that
{\psi}_{B}(y)
must be given by

{\psi}_{B}(y)=\frac{1}{2(B+1)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right).

Differentiating with respect to
B
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\psi}_{B}(y)=-\frac{1}{(B+1)^{3}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)-\frac{(1-e^{-2y})}{2(B+1)^{3}(B+e^{-2y})}<0\,\forall(B,y)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,1),

(4.4)

which establishes property (i) of
Lemma
2.2
. To establish
property (ii) we observe that

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}y}{\psi}_{B}(y)=\frac{e^{-2y}}{(B+1)^{2}(B+e^{-2y})}>0,\,\qquad\forall(B,y)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,1),

which completes the proof.
∎

5
Analysis of the diffusion phase

In this section we turn to the proofs of our results for the diffusion phase.
For didactic reasons, we shall divide them according to the type
of environment considered.

5.1
Diffusion in constant environment

In this section, we aim to prove
Theorem
2.3

regarding the weak convergence of the Wright-Fisher model in a constant
environment described in
Section
2.1
, and
Theorem
2.5
regarding the
fixation probability of the mutant. We begin with
the proof of Theorem
2.3
.
Although this theorem can be understood as a particular case of the
corresponding result
in a slowly changing environment, the proof is more digestible if
presented in this setting first.

Recall that the discrete process is represented by the vector-valued process

(\boldsymbol{X}^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbf{N}_{0}}=(X^{N}_{0}(t),X^{N}_{1}(t),\ldots,X^{N}_{K}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}},

where
X^{N}_{i}(t)
indicates the number of mutant individuals living in generation
t-i
. As explained in Section
2.1.2
, we consider the scaled vector-valued process
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
2.9
).
Our aim is to write the dynamics of
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in such a way that we can apply Katzenberger’s results explained in Section
3.1
. We will follow closely the ideas introduced by Katzenberger in
[
36
, Section 8]
.

Observe that by construction the process
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
has sample paths in
[0,1]^{K+1}
, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\left\{{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a set of i.i.d. random variables taking values in the space of measurable functions from
[0,1]^{K+1}
to
\{0,1\}
such that for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{K+1}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})\right]=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{(1-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}.

(5.1)

For fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
j\in[N]
,
conditional on
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}({\tau})
,

{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}({\tau}))
is a Bernoulli random variable
that is equal to
1
if the
j
-th individual of generation
{\tau}+1
is a
mutant. Therefore, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we
can write
X^{N}_{0}({\tau}+1)
as

X^{N}_{0}({\tau}+1)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}^{N}({\tau})}{N}\right).

In order to capture the fluctuations of the system, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\left\{{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
be a set of i.i.d. random variables taking values in the space of measurable functions from
[0,1]^{K+1}
to
\mathbb{R}
given by

{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left({\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{(1-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}\right).

(5.2)

Recall the map
\mathbf{F}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
defined in (
2.12
). We shall now describe the dynamics of
x^{N}_{0}
in terms of
{\theta}^{N}
and the map
\mathbf{F}
. By the definition of
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in (
2.9
), we have for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle x^{N}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=x^{N}_{0}(0)+\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\;\Bigg(\Bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\zeta}^{N,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N))\Bigg)-x^{N}_{0}(t/N)\Bigg)

(5.3)

\displaystyle=x^{N}_{0}(0)+M^{N}_{0}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}F_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor,

where the càdlàg process
(M^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is given by, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle M^{N}_{0}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}{\theta}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N))=\int_{0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,d{\theta}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N)).

(5.4)

Heuristically,
M^{N}_{0}
corresponds to the martingale term arising from
random sampling of genetic types at each generation, while the integral of

F_{0}
corresponds to the large drift that will force the process in the limit
to have sample paths in the diagonal
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
of the
(K+1)
-dimensional
hypercube
[0,1]^{K+1}
. Observe also that, since
X^{N}_{i}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X^{N}_{0}(t-i)
,
we can write, for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
and all

T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

x^{N}_{i}(t)=x^{N}_{i}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}F_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N})\,d\lfloor N{\tau}\rfloor.

(5.5)

As explained in Section
3.1
, in
order to prove the desired convergence, we must verify two conditions: that
the map
\mathbf{F}
is (sufficiently) asymptotically stable
(i.e. that
\mathbf{F}
satisfies Assumption
3
),
and that
M^{N}_{0}
is sufficiently well-behaved
(i.e.
M^{N}_{0}
must satisfy
Assumption
4
).
By Lemma
3.4
,
proved in Section
3.4
, the
map
\mathbf{F}
satisfies Assumption
3
,
and the diagonal
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
of the
(K+1)
-dimensional hypercube is an attractor
manifold. It remains then to establish that
M^{N}_{0}
satisfies
Assumption
4
. Since we
will need to repeatedly establish the uniform integrability of functions of
purely discontinuous martingales, we state a lemma with conditions that
are easily verified in our scenario.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Big((M^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued càdlàg martingales satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, jumps of
(M^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
can only occur at times
\{jN^{-1}:\,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
, i.e.

|M^{N}(t)-M^{N}(t-)|>0\Rightarrow t\in\{jN^{-1}:\,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

(ii)

For any
T\in[0,\infty)
, there exist
C_{T,1},C_{T,2}>0
such that the following relations hold almost surely for every
N\in\mathbb{N}

\langle M^{N}\rangle(T)\leq C_{T,1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;|M^{N}(t+1/N)-M^{N}(t)|\leq C_{T,2}.

Then the sequences of random variables
\Big((M^{N}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left[M^{N}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable, for any
T\in[0,\infty)
.

The proof of Lemma
5.1
is standard, and we postpone it until Section
6.3
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the process
M^{N}_{0}
is a square-integrable martingale. Furthermore, for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

Proof.

By (
5.2
),
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\{{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
is a set of
i.i.d. mean
0
random variables, and therefore
M^{N}_{0}
is a martingale
with respect to the natural filtration of the process

\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{T}\}_{T\geq 0}
given by
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\sigma}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t):\,t\leq T)
. Indeed, for any

0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}-\frac{1}{N}
, we have, by the tower property, that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\theta}^{N,t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t_{2}))\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t_{1}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[{\theta}^{N,t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t_{2}))\Big|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t_{2}}\right]\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t_{1}}\right]=0,

so that
M^{N}_{0}
is a
\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{T}\}
-martingale. To complete the proof, observe that by the definition of
M^{N}_{0}
in (
5.4
), and by (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), the predictable bracket process
(\langle M^{N}_{0}\rangle(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies for all
T\geq 0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
the following relation almost surely

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}_{0}\rangle(T)

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}{(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N))+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}{(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N))+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{4}.

Also, note that by the definition of the collection of random variables
\left\{{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
introduced before (
5.1
), the following relation holds almost surely for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\in[N]
and
{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in[0,1]^{K+1}}\Bigg|{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{(1-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}\Bigg|\leq 1,

and therefore (
5.4
) and (
5.2
) imply that for any
T\geq 0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{0}(t)|\leq 1.

(5.7)

Hence, (
5.6
), (
5.7
) and Lemma
5.1
imply that the sequences
\Big((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable, which completes the proof.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
2.3
.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

From the dynamics of
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
given in (
5.3
) and (
5.5
), and by Lemmas
3.4
and
5.2
, we conclude that the sequence of stochastic processes
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},M^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies Assumptions
3
and
4
. Therefore, Theorem
3.1
is applicable, which means the sequence
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},M^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight and any limiting process
(\boldsymbol{x},M_{0})
is a continuous semimartingale such that
\boldsymbol{x}(T)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
for all
T\geq 0
almost surely, where
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the diagonal of the hypercube. In particular, by the uniform integrability of the sequence
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
stated in Lemma
5.2
, any limiting process
M_{0}
is also a martingale. To characterise its quadratic variation process, we first define, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
, the process
(Q^{N}(t))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
by

\displaystyle Q^{N}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{(1-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\left(1-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{(1-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\right)\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N)

(5.8)

\displaystyle=(M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0})\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)}{\left((1-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)^{2}}\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N).

Then by (
5.4
),
(Q^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. By Lemma
5.2
,
\left((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable, and therefore, taking
N\rightarrow\infty
in (
5.8
) and recalling that on the diagonal
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
of the hypercube,
x_{0}=\ldots=x_{K}
, we conclude that the process
(Q(t))_{t\geq 0}
given by

Q(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=M_{0}(T)^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,dt

is also a martingale, i.e.
[M_{0}](T)=\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,dt
, for all
T\geq 0
. Hence, there exists a standard Brownian motion
W_{0}
adapted to the natural filtration of
(\boldsymbol{x},M_{0})
such that for all
T\geq 0
,

M_{0}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}\,dW_{0}(t).

Recalling that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the projection map associated to the ODE with vector field
\mathbf{F}
, we conclude
from Theorem
3.1
that for all
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle x_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}\sqrt{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}\,dW_{0}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}\,dt.

(5.9)

The proof is then complete by applying Lemma
3.5
to (
5.9
).
∎

We can now establish Theorem
2.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

We start by establishing that the fixation probability is bounded by the map
(B,y)\mapsto{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)
defined in (
2.26
). Since we do not have a closed expression for the second order derivative
\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}
which holds for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we will use property
(ii)
of Proposition
2.4
, i.e. that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x})\leq\frac{B(B(1-x_{0})+2)}{(B(1-x_{0})+1)^{3}}.

Therefore, instead of analysing directly (
2.19
), we consider the alternative SDE

\tilde{x}_{0}(T)=\;{x}_{0}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\tilde{x}_{0}(1-\tilde{x}_{0})\frac{B(B(1-\tilde{x}_{0})+2)}{(B(1-\tilde{x}_{0})+1)^{3}}\right)\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{x}_{0}(1-\tilde{x}_{0})}}{(B(1-\tilde{x}_{0})+1)}\,dW_{0}(t),

(5.10)

starting from the same initial condition as
x_{0}
. Note that to bound the fixation probability of
(x_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, it will suffice to bound the fixation probability of
(\tilde{x}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. For any
a\in[0,1]
, recall the definition of
T_{a}
in (
2.25
), and define the stopping time

\widetilde{T}_{a}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,\tilde{x}_{0}(t)=a\}.

Then, we have for any
y\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(T_{1}<T_{0}\,\Big|\,x_{0}(0)={\psi}_{B}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{T}_{1}<\widetilde{T}_{0}\,\Big|\,\tilde{x}_{0}(0)={\psi}_{B}(y)\right).

(5.11)

The right hand side is most easily calulated using the scale function for
the diffusion.
Writing the SDE in (
5.10
) as

d\tilde{x}_{0}={\mu}_{0}(\tilde{x}_{0})\,dt+{\eta}_{0}(\tilde{x}_{0})\,dW_{0}(t),

the scale function
S:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
can be written

S(v)=\int_{0}^{v}\exp\left(-2\int_{0}^{w}\frac{{\mu}_{0}(z)}{{\eta}_{0}^{2}(z)}\,dz\right)\,dw,

(5.12)

where

-2\frac{{\mu}_{0}(z)}{{\eta}_{0}^{2}(z)}=-\frac{B(B(1-z)+2)}{(B(1-z)+1)},\;\qquad\forall z\in[0,1].

Hence, for any
w\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle-2\int_{0}^{w}\frac{{\mu}_{0}(z)}{{\eta}_{0}^{2}(z)}\,dz

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{w}\frac{B(B(1-z)+2)}{(B(1-z)+1)}dz

\displaystyle=-B\int_{0}^{w}\left(1+\frac{1}{(B(1-z)+1)}\right)dz

\displaystyle=-Bw-\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B(1-w)+1}\right).

Substituting in (
5.12
), for any
v\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle S(v)=\frac{1}{(B+1)}\int_{0}^{v}e^{-Bw}(B(1-w)+1)\,dw=\frac{1}{(B+1)}\left({1-e^{-Bv}+ve^{-Bv}}\right).

(5.13)

Thus, (see e.g.
[
28
, Equation (4.17)]
), for any
y\in[0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{T}_{1}<\widetilde{T}_{0}\,\Big|\,\tilde{x}_{0}(0)={\psi}_{B}(y)\right)=\frac{S({\psi}_{B}(y))-S(0)}{S(1)-S(0)}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y),

(5.14)

where the last equality follows
from (
5.13
) and the definition of

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)
in (
2.26
). The
bound on the fixation probability stated in
Theorem
2.5
then follows from
applying (
5.14
)
to (
5.11
).

To conclude our proof, it remains to establish the properties of the map
B\mapsto{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)
, for any
y\in(0,1)
. By the definition of
{\psi}_{B}
in Lemma
2.2
, we have that for any
y\in[0,1)
,

\lim_{B\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\psi}_{B}(y)=y\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{B\rightarrow\infty}{\psi}_{B}(y)=0.

Since by (
5.13
), the function
v\mapsto S(v)
is continuous,
we conclude that for any
y\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{B\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)=y\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{B\rightarrow\infty}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)=0.

(5.15)

It remains to establish that the map
B\mapsto{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)
is strictly decreasing for any
y\in(0,1)
. By (
2.26
), we have for any
y\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)=\left({\psi}_{B}(y)+B\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\psi}_{B}(y)+\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\psi}_{B}(y)+{\psi}_{B}^{2}(y)+B{\psi}_{B}(y)\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\psi}_{B}(y)\right)e^{-B{\psi}_{B}(y)}.

(5.16)

By applying Lemma
2.2
and (
4.4
) to (
5.16
) and rearranging terms, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-B{\psi}_{B}(y)}}{2(B+1)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)\left[\frac{(1-B)}{2(B+1)^{3}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)-1\right]

(5.17)

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{-B{\psi}_{B}(y)}(1-e^{-2y})}{2(B+1)^{3}(B+e^{-2y})}\left[B+1+\frac{1}{2(B+1)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)\right].

In order to prove that the map
B\mapsto{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)
is strictly decreasing for any
y\in(0,1)
, it will suffice to establish that

\frac{(1-B)}{2(B+1)^{3}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)\leq 1\quad\forall(B,y)\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1).

(5.18)

Since for any fixed
y\in(0,1)
, the map

B\mapsto\frac{(1-B)}{2(B+1)^{3}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right),

is strictly decreasing in
B
, we conclude that for any
y\in(0,1)
and
B\in[0,\infty)
,

\frac{(1-B)}{2(B+1)^{3}}\log\left(\frac{B+1}{B+e^{-2y}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\log(e^{-2y})=y<1,

and therefore (
5.18
) holds. By applying (
5.18
) to (
5.17
), we conclude that for any
y\in(0,1)
, and any
B\in[0,\infty)
, we have

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}B}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}(B,y)<0.

(5.19)

Hence, Theorem
2.5
follows from applying (
5.14
) to (
5.11
), and then from (
5.15
) and (
5.19
).
∎

5.2
Diffusion in slowly changing environment

In this section, we prove the weak convergence of the Wright-Fisher model
with seed bank with a slowly changing population size, i.e. we prove
Theorem
2.6
. Our arguments will
be closely related to those in
Section
5.1
.
In contrast to the case of constant population size, we must now control two
sources of noise:
that arising from the random process of sampling the genetic types in
each generation, and the environmental noise.

Recall
that
({\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
records the change in
the population size over time, and,
from (
2.9
), that

x^{N}(t)=X^{N}\left(\lfloor Nt\rfloor\right)/N
and

{\xi}^{N}(t)={\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}\left(\lfloor Nt\rfloor\right)
.
By Assumption
1
, there exist functions
{\alpha},{\eta}:[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
so that as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
{\xi}^{N}
converges weakly to the diffusion

d{\xi}={\alpha}({\xi})\,dt+{\eta}({\xi})\,dW_{\textrm{env}},

where
W_{\textrm{env}}
is a standard Brownian motion. To introduce the proof, we define

\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{({\xi},x_{0},\ldots,x_{K})\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\times\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)^{K+1}:\;{\xi}\geq x_{0}\textrm{ and }{\xi}_{\max}\geq x_{i}\;\forall\,i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket\right\}.

(5.20)

By construction the process
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N})

has sample paths in
\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}
, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Following the same strategy as was used in the proof of
Theorem
2.3
, we shall
define

\{0,1\}
-valued random variables that record, for each individual
in a new generation, whether or not it is a mutant.
In contrast to the case of constant environment, the parameters of these
Bernoulli-simile random variables will depend not only on

\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
, but also on
{\xi}^{N}
.

Formally, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\left\{{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a set
of i.i.d. random variables taking values in the space of measurable functions
from
\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}
to
\{0,1\}
such that for all

(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\in\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\right]=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{({\xi}-x_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}.

(5.21)

Note that the denominator in (
5.21
)
differs from that in (
5.1
)
because when the carrying capacity is not kept constant, the number of wild
type individuals (divided by
N
) in the population is given by
{\xi}-x_{0}

instead of
1-x_{0}
. The other terms are the same because we are keeping
track of the number of mutants in each generation, rather than the proportion.
Then, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we can write

X^{N}_{0}(t+1)
as

X^{N}_{0}(t+1)=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor{\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}(t+1)N\rfloor}{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t)}{N},{\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}(t)\right).

(5.22)

As in the proof of Theorem
2.3
, in order to study the fluctuations arising from the genetic sampling, we introduce for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of i.i.d. random variables
\left\{{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
taking values in the space of measurable functions from
\mathscr{U}
to
\mathbb{R}
given by

{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor{\xi}N\rfloor}\left({\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{({\xi}-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}\right)

(5.23)

In order to apply Theorem
3.1
, we must describe the dynamics of
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
and
{\xi}^{N}
in terms of semimartingales and a forcing flow. Starting with
{\xi}^{N}
, we have that for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}(t)={\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}(0)+\sum_{{\tau}=0}^{t-1}\frac{{\alpha}({\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}({\tau}))}{N}+\sum_{{\tau}=0}^{t-1}\left({\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}({\tau}+1)-{\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}({\tau})-\frac{{\alpha}({\mathord{\hbox{\char 4\relax}}}^{N}({\tau}))}{N}\right),

where
{\alpha}:[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the continuous function satisfying Assumption
1
and that corresponds to the drift of the limiting environmental diffusion process
{\xi}
. Hence, recalling (
2.28
), we have for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle{\xi}^{N}(T)

\displaystyle={\xi}^{N}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}{\alpha}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)\,d\left({\lfloor Nt\rfloor}/{N}\right)+M_{\textrm{env}}^{N}(T)

(5.24)

\displaystyle={\xi}^{N}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}{\alpha}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)\,d\left({\lfloor Nt\rfloor}/{N}\right)+M_{\textrm{env}}^{N}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}F^{\,\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor,

where the process
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
is given by, for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

M_{\textrm{env}}^{N}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{{\tau}=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)-{\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)-\frac{1}{N}{{\alpha}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)}\right),

(5.25)

and
F^{\,\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}\equiv 0
is the coordinate indicating the impact of the forcing flow field
\mathbf{F}^{{\,\textrm{sl,env}}}
, given in (
2.32
), on the environment.
The forcing field does not affect the population size
and
M_{\textrm{env}}^{N}
represents the noise arising from the environmental dynamics. We also emphasise that, as expected, the process
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
does not appear in the dynamics of
{\xi}^{N}
. This reflects our underlying assumption that the population size is not influenced by the genetic composition of the population in this model.

We now describe the dynamics of
x^{N}_{0}
. Recall the definition of the
flow field
\mathbf{F}^{\textrm{sl,env}}:\mathscr{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+2}
in (
2.32
). Following the same strategy as we used to derive (
5.3
), we write
x^{N}_{0}
in terms of the sums of the family of random variables
{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}
, i.e. we write, for any
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle x^{N}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=x^{N}_{0}(0)+\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\;\Bigg(\Bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor N{\xi}^{N}((t+1)/N)\rfloor}{\zeta}^{N,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N))\Bigg)-x^{N}_{0}(t/N)\Bigg)

(5.26)

\displaystyle=x^{N}_{0}(0)+M^{N}_{0}(T)+\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+{M}^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi}^{N})\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{({\xi}^{N}-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\,d\left(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}F^{\,\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N})\,d\lfloor N{\tau}\rfloor,

where the processes
(M^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
,
(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
and
({M}^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
are given by, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle M^{N}_{0}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,d{\theta}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N)),

(5.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}{({\xi}^{N}(t/N)-x^{N}_{0}(t/N))+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t/N)}\Bigg)\,dM^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t/N),

(5.28)

\displaystyle M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(T)

\displaystyle\quad\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\Bigg(\mathds{1}_{\left\{{\xi}^{N}(t/N)<{\xi}^{N}((t+1)/N)\right\}}\sum_{j=\left\lfloor N{\xi}^{N}(t/N)\right\rfloor+1}^{\left\lfloor N{\xi}^{N}((t+1)/N)\right\rfloor}{\beta}_{j}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\mathds{1}_{\left\{{\xi}^{N}((t+1)/N)<{\xi}^{N}(t/N)\right\}}\sum_{j=\left\lfloor N{\xi}^{N}((t+1)/N)\right\rfloor+1}^{\left\lfloor N{\xi}^{N}(t/N)\right\rfloor}{\beta}_{j}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N))\Bigg).

(5.29)

In (
5.28
),
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}

is as defined in (
5.25
), and for all

N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
and

(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\in\mathscr{U}
,
{\beta}^{N,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})

in (
5.29
)
is given by

{\beta}^{N,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\zeta}^{N,t}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{({\xi}-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}.

(5.30)

Finally, for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, since
X^{N}_{i}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=X^{N}_{0}(t-i)
, we can write, for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
and all
t\geq 0
,

x^{N}_{i}(t)=x^{N}_{i}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}F^{\,\textrm{sl,env}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N})\,d\lfloor N{\tau}\rfloor.

(5.31)

The three fluctuation terms
M_{0}^{N}
,
\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}^{N}
, and

M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
account, respectively, for the intrinsic randomness due
to sampling in the Wright-Fisher model, the fluctuations due to changes in
population size, and fluctuations due to
both
changes in population
size and competition with wild type individuals.
Our next result shows that these processes capture the randomness of our Wright-Fisher model.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the processes
M^{N}_{0}
and
{M}^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
are square integrable martingales. Moreover, there exist processes
({\varepsilon}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(\tilde{{\varepsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, such that
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}
and
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+\tilde{{\varepsilon}}^{N}
are square integrable martingales for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and such that for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[|{\varepsilon}^{N}(T)|^{p}\right]\rightarrow 0
and
\mathbb{E}\left[|\tilde{{\varepsilon}}^{N}(T)|^{p}\right]\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, for all
p\geq 1
. Furthermore, for all
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(\left[\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+\tilde{{\varepsilon}}^{N}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\Big((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big((M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+{\varepsilon}^{N}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

Proof.

We shall study each of the processes in the statement of the lemma separately.

Step
(i)
: Characterisation of
M^{N}_{0}
:

Note that by (
5.23
), for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left\{{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
is a set of i.i.d. mean
0
random variables, and therefore
M^{N}_{0}
is a martingale. To establish the required uniform integrability, we first note that (
5.27
), (
5.21
) and (
5.23
) imply that the following relation holds almost surely for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in[0,\infty)
:

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}_{0}\rangle(T)

(5.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\left(\frac{\lfloor{\xi}^{N}N\rfloor}{N}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{({\xi}^{N}-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{({\xi}^{N}-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\right)\right)(t/N)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T{\xi}_{\max}}{4}.

Moreover, by the same argument as was used to
derive (
5.7
),
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in[0,\infty)
, the following relation holds almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;|M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{0}(t)|\leq 1.

(5.33)

Combining (
5.32
) with (
5.33
), and applying Lemma
5.1
, we conclude that the sequences of random variables
\Big((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

Step
(ii)
: Characterisation of
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
:

Let
\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the natural filtration of
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),{\xi}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Then, by the definition of
{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}
given in (
5.21
) and the definition of
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
in (
5.29
), we conclude that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t)\,|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\times{\sigma}({\xi}^{N}(t+1/N))\right]=0.

Thus, by the tower property of conditional expectations,
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
is a martingale. Moreover, since the collection of random variables
\left\{{\zeta}^{N,t}_{j}:\,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
is conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t))^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\times{\sigma}({\xi}^{N}(t+1/N))\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2}\sum_{j=\lfloor{\xi}_{\min}N\rfloor}^{\lfloor{\xi}_{\max}N\rfloor}\mathds{1}_{\left\{N\left({\xi}^{N}(T)\wedge{\xi}^{N}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right)\right)\leq j\leq N\left({\xi}^{N}(t)\vee{\xi}^{N}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right)\right)\right\}}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\Bigg({\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),{\xi}^{N}(t))-\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t)}{({\xi}^{N}(t)-x^{N}_{0}(t))+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t)}\Bigg)^{2}\Bigg|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\times{\sigma}({\xi}^{N}(t+1/N))\Bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}\left|{\xi}^{N}(t+1/N)-{\xi}^{N}(t)\right|.

Hence, by the tower property, for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t)\right)^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]

(5.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|{\xi}^{N}(t+1/N)-{\xi}^{N}(t)\right||\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\xi}^{N}(t+1/N)-{\xi}^{N}(t)\right)^{2}|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq{||{\eta}||_{\infty}^{1/2}}N^{-3/2}+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{-3/2-{\delta}}\right)

for some
{\delta}>0
, where we applied Cauchy-Schwarz to obtain the
second and third inequalities, and
Assumption
1
on the dynamics
of the environment for the last one.
Since
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
is a purely discontinuous martingale, we conclude that the following estimate holds almost surely for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
T\geq 0

\langle M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}\rangle(T)=\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\mathbb{E}\left[(M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t))^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]\leq T{||{\eta}||_{\infty}^{1/2}}N^{-1/2}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2-{\delta}}),

(5.35)

where the last inequality follows from (
5.34
). Moreover, from the definition of
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
in (
5.29
) and from Assumption
1
, we have that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the following estimate holds almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}|M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(t-)|\leq{\xi}_{\max}-{\xi}_{\min}.

(5.36)

Therefore, (
5.35
), (
5.36
) and Lemma
5.1
imply that
\Big(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable for every
T\geq 0
, as desired.

Step
(iii)
: Characterisation of
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
and of
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
:

Let
w^{N}_{1},w^{N}_{2},w^{N}_{3}:[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the maps satisfying Assumption
1
, and for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let the process
({\varepsilon}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be given by, for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

{\varepsilon}^{N}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}w^{N}_{1}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right).

(5.37)

Then, by Assumption
1
,
\left((M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. Moreover, by applying the triangle inequality and Assumption
1
to (
5.37
), we conclude that there exists
{\delta}>0
such that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in[0,\infty)
, the following relation holds almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|{\varepsilon}^{N}(t)|\lesssim TN^{-{\delta}}.

(5.38)

Observe also that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, Assumption
1
and (
5.25
) imply that almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\,|(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})(t+1/N)-(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})(t)|\leq{\xi}_{\max}-{\xi}_{\min}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}).

(5.39)

Moreover, (
5.25
), (
5.37
) and Assumption
1
imply that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big((M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})(t+1/N)-(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})(t)\Big)^{2}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\Big]\leq||{\eta}||_{\infty}N^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1-{\delta}}),

and therefore

\langle M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\rangle(T)\leq T||{\eta}||_{\infty}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-{\delta}})\;\textrm{almost surely}.

(5.40)

It follows from (
5.39
), (
5.40
) and Lemma
5.1
that, for all
T\in[0,\infty)
, both the sequences of random variables
\Big((M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

The analogous results concerning the process
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
, defined in (
5.28
), hold due to the fact that the process

\left(\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t-)}{{\xi}^{N}(t-)-x^{N}_{0}(t-)+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}(t-)}\right)_{t\geq 0}

is predictable with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
introduced before (
2.30
), and then by observing that the map

(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\mapsto\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}{({\xi}-x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x_{i}}

(5.41)

is uniformly bounded by 1. The proof is then complete.
∎

We now characterise the evolution of
{\xi}^{N}
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{N}
in terms of a well-behaved semimartingale. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
K+2
-dimensional process

\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}=\left({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(t),\ldots,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{K}(t),{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}

such that, for all
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{T}{\alpha}({\xi}^{N})\,d\left({\lfloor Nt\rfloor}/{N}\right)+M_{\textrm{env}}^{N}(T),

(5.42)

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=M^{N}_{0}(T)+\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+{M}^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi}^{N})\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{{\xi}^{N}-(1-b_{0})x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\,d\left(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N\right),

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}^{N}_{i}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=0\,\textrm{ for all }i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket.

Note that, by the description of
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),{\xi}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, we can write for all
T\geq 0
,

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(T),{\xi}^{N}(T))=(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(0),{\xi}^{N}(0))+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{F}^{\textrm{sl,env}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor.

(5.43)

Our next result is a corollary of Lemma
5.3
. Recall that the total variation of a càdlàg process over the interval
[0,T]
is denoted
\mathfrak{T}(\cdot,t)
.

Corollary 5.4
.

The sequence of processes
(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{K+2}\right)
. Moreover, for any
T\geq 0
, the sequence of random variables
(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}(T))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable, and for each
T\geq 0
, the sequences of processes

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{T}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\alpha}({\xi}^{N})\,d\left({\lfloor Nt^{\prime}\rfloor}/{N}\right)\right)_{t\geq 0},T\right)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{T}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi}^{N})\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{{\xi}^{N}-(1-b_{0})x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}\,d\left(\lfloor Nt^{\prime}\rfloor/N\right)\right)_{t\geq 0},T\right)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}},

are both relatively compact in
\mathscr{D}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.3
, the fact that both
{\alpha}
and the map defined in (
5.41
) are bounded in
[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
and in
\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}
, and by standard results regarding the Skorokhod topology of
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{K+2}\right)
, see e.g.
[
27
, Theorem 3.8.6(c)]
, we conclude that
(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{K+2}\right)
. Uniform integrability also follows from Lemma
5.3
and the boundedness of
{\alpha}
and the map given in (
5.41
). Finally, for any
T\geq 0
, and any Lebesgue integrable function
g:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathfrak{T}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{t}g(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\right)_{t\geq 0},\,T\right)=\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|\,dt.

Hence, the relative compactness of the total variation processes follows from the almost surely boundedness of the integrands, as desired.
∎

Having verified
Assumption
4
, we now
turn to the forcing flow
\boldsymbol{F}^{\textrm{sl,env}}
defined
in (
2.32
) and associated to the projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}
.
Recall
the map

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
from (
2.16
),
the manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}

from (
2.34
), and
the notation

\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\boldsymbol{x}/{\xi}
,
for any
(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}
.
To emphasise that, in contrast to the case of a constant population size,

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
is defined as a function of
\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})

rather than
\boldsymbol{x}
,
we shall use the notation
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}/{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}
to indicate
differentiation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
with respect to
the
i
th component; it is
important to keep this in mind when we use
Lemma
3.5
in the proof of
our next result.

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{{\xi}}{B({\xi}-x_{0})+{\xi}}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x^{2}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{1}{{\xi}}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}^{2}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

(5.44)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=0\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{-Bx_{0}}{(B({\xi}-x_{0})+{\xi})^{2}}.

(5.45)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})

\displaystyle=1,\;\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=0,

(5.46)

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\qquad\;\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{\textrm{env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{j}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=0\qquad\;\forall\,i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}.

Proof.

Observe that (
5.46
) follows directly from (
2.35
). To prove (
5.44
), we first observe that by applying the chain rule to (
2.36
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

(5.47)

Hence, the first identity in (
5.44
) follows from applying
Lemma
3.5

to (
5.47
), while the second follows from differentiating both sides of (
5.47
) and applying the chain rule.

It remains to establish (
5.45
). By differentiating both sides
of (
2.36
) and applying the chain rule, we have

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})={\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi}))-\frac{1}{{\xi}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}x_{i}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

(5.48)

Using the fact that on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}
,

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi}))=\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}}=\frac{x_{i}}{{\xi}}\qquad\forall\,i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket,

applying Lemma
3.5
to (
5.48
), and then observing that by (
2.3
),
B=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=i}^{K}b_{i}
, we conclude that

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=0.

(5.49)

Differentiating both sides of (
5.48
) with respect to
{\xi}
, and then using that
\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\boldsymbol{x}/{\xi}
and the chain rule, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})

(5.50)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}x_{i}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi}))+\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}x_{i}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi}))+\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=0}^{K}x_{i}x_{j}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=0}^{K}x_{i}x_{j}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

To compute the right-hand side of this equation, we first observe that on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}
,
x_{0}=\cdots=x_{K}
, and therefore (
5.50
) yields

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=\left(\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{i}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

(5.51)

Applying (
3.36
), (
3.37
) and (
3.38
) to (
5.51
), we have

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=-\left(\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}\sum_{j=0}^{K}{\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{ij},

(5.52)

where
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}=\left({\theta}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}_{ij}\right)_{i,j\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}}
is defined after (
3.29
). By combining (
3.22
), (
3.16
) and (
5.52
), we conclude that

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=0.

(5.53)

It remains to establish the last identity in (
5.45
). By differentiating both sides of (
5.48
) with respect to
x_{0}
, and then using the definition of
\boldsymbol{{\rho}}

and the chain rule, we have

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})=-\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{K}x_{i}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{0}{\rho}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}(\boldsymbol{x},{\xi})).

(5.54)

Substituting (
3.36
)
and (
3.37
), the fact
that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 1\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
, and the definition
of
\boldsymbol{u}
from (
3.16
), we
conclude
from (
5.54
) that the last identity in (
5.45
) holds, which completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove our convergence result.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Our proof consists of an application of
Theorem
3.1
. Recall the definition of
the multidimensional semimartingale
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}
given
in (
5.42
), and
identity (
5.43
).
By Theorem
3.1
,
Lemma
5.3
, and
Corollary
5.4
, the sequence
of processes
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},{\xi}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),\mathscr{U}^{\textrm{sl}}\times\mathbb{R}^{K+2}\right)
. Moreover, Theorem
3.1
also implies that any
subsequential limit
(\boldsymbol{x}(t),{\xi}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a diffusion with sample paths in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\;\textrm{sl,env}}\times\mathbb{R}^{K+2}
. From now on, let
(\boldsymbol{x}(t),{\xi}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a subsequential limit. By Assumption
1
on the dynamics of the environment, it is immediate that
{\xi}
must be the (unique in law) solution to the SDE

d{\xi}={\alpha}({\xi})\,dt+{\eta}({\xi})\,dW_{\textrm{env}}(t).

To compute the drift and the noise terms of the SDE describing the dynamics of
\boldsymbol{x}
, we need to characterise the limiting process
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
in terms of
(\boldsymbol{x}(t),{\xi}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Bearing this aim in mind, we shall characterise the limit points of each of the martingales
M^{N}_{0}
,
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
and
\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}^{N}
that appear in the dynamics of the discrete process (see Equations (
5.27
), (
5.28
) and (
5.29
)). Starting with
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
, we note that by estimate (
5.34
), we have for all
t\geq 0
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}\right](T)\right]=0,

and therefore, in the limit,
M^{N}_{\textrm{aux}}
vanishes. Now, regarding
M^{N}_{0}
, we note that by Lemma
5.3
,
M^{N}_{0}
converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
to a stochastic process
M_{0}
which is also a martingale. Moreover, by (
5.27
), we have that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the process
\left(Q^{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
given by, for all
T\geq 0

\displaystyle Q^{N}(T)

(5.55)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(M^{N}_{0}(T)\right)^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)\left({\xi}^{N}-(1-b_{0})x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{\left({\xi}^{N}-(1-b_{0})x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)}\;d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N),

is a martingale. Then, by Lemma
5.3
, we have uniform integrability for the sequence
\left((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, and therefore, taking
N\rightarrow\infty
in (
5.55
) and recalling that on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\textrm{sl,env}}
,
x_{0}=\ldots=x_{K}
, we conclude that the process
(Q(t))_{t\geq 0}
given by

Q(T)=M_{0}(T)^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}{\xi}_{s}\left(\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}}-\frac{x_{0}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\right)\,dt,

is also a martingale. This characterises the quadratic variation of
M_{0}
and we deduce that there exists a standard Brownian motion
W_{0}
such that

M_{0}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}({\xi}-x_{0})}{{\xi}}}\,dW_{0}(t).

(5.56)

By identities (
5.25
)
and (
5.28
), we can write

\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
as a stochastic integral

\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}}{\left(({\xi}^{N}-x^{N}_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{K}b_{i}x^{N}_{i}\right)}dM^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t).

Using Lemma
5.3
, the convergence
result stated in Theorem 7.2 in
[
36
]
, and
the fact that on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,
x_{0}=\ldots=x_{K}
, we deduce that in the
limit,
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
converges to a martingale

\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}
that can be written as

\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,d\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\eta}({\xi})\,dW_{\textrm{env}}(t^{\prime})\right)=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{x_{0}}{{\xi}_{s}}{\eta}({\xi})\,dW_{\textrm{env}}(t).

We must now establish that the Brownian motions
W_{0}
and

W_{\textrm{env}}
are independent. By Knight’s theorem, it is enough to
establish that in the limit, the covariation process satisfies almost surely,
for any
T\geq 0
,

\left[M_{0},M_{\textrm{env}}\right]\equiv 0.

(5.57)

Let
{\varepsilon}^{N}
be the process defined in (
5.37
). By the definition of
M^{N}_{0}
and
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
in (
5.27
) and in (
5.25
), respectively, we have for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle[M^{N}_{0},M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}](T)

(5.58)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}{\theta}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N))\Bigg({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)-{\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)-\frac{1}{N}{{\alpha}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle-w^{N}_{1}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg).

Since for any
t\geq 0
,

{\theta}^{N,t}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t/N),{\xi}^{N}(t/N))\;\textrm{and}\;\Bigg({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)-{\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)-\frac{1}{N}{{\alpha}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)}-w^{N}_{1}\left({\xi}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg)

are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
, it follows that
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0},M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}\right](t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
. By Lemma
5.3
, both
(M^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy Assumption
4
, and therefore Proposition 4.3 of
[
36
]
implies that the sequence of càdlàg processes
\Big(\left(\left[M^{N}_{0},M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}\right](t)\right)_{t\geq 0}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges weakly in
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
to
\Big([M_{0},M_{\textrm{env}}](t)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
as
N\rightarrow 0
. By the uniform integrability of the sequences
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\left(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}+{\varepsilon}^{N}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
stated for all
T\geq 0
in Lemma
5.3
, we conclude that
\Big([M_{0},M_{\textrm{env}}](t)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
is also a martingale. Since
M_{0}
and
M_{\textrm{env}}
are continuous,
\Big([M_{0},M_{\textrm{env}}](t)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
is a continuous finite variation process which is also a martingale, i.e. (
5.57
) holds. This concludes the proof that
W_{0}
and
W_{\textrm{env}}
are independent Brownian motions. Therefore, from (
5.42
), the process
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{0}
is given by, for all
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=M_{0}(T)+\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi})x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,dt

(5.59)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}({\xi}-x_{0})}{{\xi}_{s}}}\,dW_{0}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\eta}({\xi}_{s})x_{0}}{{\xi}_{s}}\,dW_{\textrm{env}}({{\tau}})+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi})x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,dt.

Finally, by Theorem
3.1
, we conclude that the limiting process
(x_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies the SDE

\displaystyle x_{0}(T)-x_{0}(0)

(5.60)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{x_{0}({\xi}-x_{0})}{{\xi}}}\,dW_{0}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}\frac{{\eta}({\xi})x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,dW_{\textrm{env}}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}\frac{{\alpha}({\xi})x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,dt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}\frac{x_{0}({\xi}-x_{0})}{{\xi}}\,dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}\frac{{\eta}^{2}({\xi})x^{2}_{0}}{{\xi}^{2}}\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\textrm{sl,env}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\xi}}\frac{{\eta}^{2}({\xi})x_{0}}{{\xi}}\,dt.

Applying Lemma
5.5
to (
5.60
) completes the proof.
∎

We now proceed to the proof of
Proposition
2.8
, which gives a qualitative description of the impact of fluctuations in the population size on the evolution of the dormancy trait.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

We will divide the proof into steps corresponding to each of the assertions (i) - (iv).

Proof of assertion (i):

The fact that
g(\boldsymbol{b},0,{\xi})\equiv 0
follows directly from (
2.43
), while the fact that
g(\boldsymbol{b},1,{\xi})\equiv 0
follows from applying Proposition
2.4
(iii) to (
2.43
).

Proof of assertion (ii):

Observe that for every
K\in\mathbb{N}
, all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}^{*}
and all
0<{\xi}_{\min}<{\xi}_{\max}
, we have for all
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]

\displaystyle\lim_{{\rho}_{0}\rightarrow 0}\frac{B(1-{\rho}_{0})}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1){\xi}}+\frac{{\rho}_{0}}{2}\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\rho}_{0}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\rho}}\right)-\frac{2B}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1)}\right)=\frac{B}{(B+1){\xi}}\geq\frac{B}{(B+1){\xi}_{\max}}>0.

Hence, the result follows by the continuity of the map
g:\mathbb{S}^{*}_{K}\times[0,1]\times[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined in (
2.43
).

Proof of assertion (iii):

By applying Proposition
2.4
(ii) to (
2.43
), we conclude that

\displaystyle g(\boldsymbol{b},{\rho}_{0},{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq{\rho}_{0}\left[\frac{B(1-{\rho}_{0})}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1){\xi}}+\frac{{\rho}_{0}}{2}\left(\frac{B(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+2)}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1)^{3}}-\frac{2B}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1)}\right)\right]

(5.61)

\displaystyle=\frac{B{\rho}_{0}(1-{\rho}_{0})}{(B(1-{\rho}_{0})+1)^{3}{\xi}}\left[B^{2}(1-{\rho}_{0})(1-{\rho}_{0}(1+{\xi}))+B\left(2-{\rho}_{0}\left(2+\frac{3{\xi}}{2}\right)\right)+1\right].

Let

{\rho}_{c}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{4+{\xi}_{\min}}{4+3{\xi}_{\min}}.

(5.62)

To establish assertion (iii), we will first prove that for
B
sufficiently large, the term on the right-hand side of (
5.61
) is negative for
{\rho}_{0}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
. Since
{\xi}_{\min}>0
,
{\rho}_{c}>1/(1+{\xi}_{\min})\geq 1/(1+{\xi})
, for any
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
, and therefore for
{\rho}_{0}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
, we have

B^{2}(1-{\rho}_{0})(1-{\rho}_{0}(1+{\xi}))<0.

(5.63)

Moreover, for
{\rho}_{0}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
and
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
, we have

2-{\rho}_{0}\left(2+\frac{3{\xi}}{2}\right)\leq-\frac{{\xi}_{\min}}{(4+3{\xi}_{\min})}\left(2+\frac{3{\xi}}{2}\right)\leq-\frac{{\xi}_{\min}}{(4+3{\xi}_{\min})}\left(2+\frac{3{\xi}_{\min}}{2}\right)=-\frac{{\xi}_{\min}}{2}.

(5.64)

Let
B_{c}=2/{\xi}_{\min}
. Then for
B>B_{c}
, (
5.64
) implies that for
{\rho}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
, we have

B\left(2-{\rho}_{0}\left(2+\frac{3{\xi}}{2}\right)\right)+1<0.

(5.65)

Hence, by applying (
5.63
) and (
5.65
) to (
5.61
), we conclude that if
B>B_{c}
and
{\rho}_{0}\in({\rho}_{c},1)
, then
g(\boldsymbol{b},{\rho}_{0},{\xi})<0
for all
{\xi}\in[{\xi}_{\min},{\xi}_{\max}]
. Since the map
{\xi}\in(0,\infty)\mapsto B_{c}=2/{\xi}
is decreasing and satisfies the limit in (
2.44
), the proof is complete.
∎

5.3
Diffusion in fast-changing environment

In this section, we prove
Theorem
2.10
.
Our arguments will be closely related to the ones used in
Sections
5.1

and
5.2
. As in the
proof of Theorem
2.6
, we will
construct Bernoulli-simile random variables that will
record whether or not each individual in a new generation is a mutant.
In the case of a fast changing environment, the outcome probability depends on
the genetic composition of the previous
K
generations, the history of the
environment in the previous
K
generations, and on the current environment. Formally, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\left\{{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in[N]\right\}
be a set of i.i.d. random variables taking values in the space of measurable functions from
[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K+1}
to
\{0,1\}
such that for all
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\times[-1,1]
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\right]=\frac{b_{0}(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}{\left((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}\right)(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}.

(5.66)

By (
2.47
), we can construct our Wright-Fisher process in a fast-changing environment in such a way that for any
t\in(0,\infty)
and every
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lfloor Nt\rfloor\geq 1
,

x^{N}_{0}(t)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\zeta}_{j}^{N,(\lfloor Nt\rfloor-1)}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(t-\tfrac{1}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(t-\tfrac{1}{N}\right),{\upsilon}_{0}^{N}\left(t\right)\right).

(5.67)

Our next result is an elementary moment computation, which will be used in our description of the moments of increments of
x^{N}_{0}
. For every

N\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
{\pi}^{N}
for the distribution of
{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}(0)

which, by Assumption
2
,
takes the form

{\pi}^{N}(\{-s_{N}\})={\pi}^{N}(\{s_{N}\})=\frac{1-{\pi}^{N}(\{0\})}{2}=p\in[0,1/2],

(5.68)

where
(s_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies (
2.45
).

Lemma 5.6
.

For constants
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}\in[0,1]
with
f_{1}\geq f_{2}
and
f_{3}\geq f_{4}>0
, and for
\bar{{\upsilon}}
a random variable with distribution
{\pi}^{N}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]=\frac{f_{1}}{f_{3}}+\frac{2ps_{N}^{2}f_{4}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{(f_{3}^{2}-s_{N}^{2}f_{4}^{2})f_{3}},

(5.69)

and

\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\left(\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}-\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{2ps_{N}^{2}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})^{2}}{f_{3}^{4}}+\mathcal{O}(s_{N}^{3}).

(5.70)

Moreover, for any
q\geq 3
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\left|\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}-\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]\right|^{q}\right]=\mathcal{O}(s_{N}^{q}).

(5.71)

Furthermore,

\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\bar{{\upsilon}}\left(\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}-\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]\right)\right]=-2ps_{N}^{2}\frac{(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(s_{N}^{3}).

(5.72)

The proof of Lemma
5.6
is
elementary, and we postpone it to
Section
6.4
.
We now show that
Lemma
2.9
,
which describes the expected increments over a single generation in the
environment and in the proportion of mutants, is a corollary of
Lemma
5.6
.

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

Identities (
2.51
) and (
2.52
) follow directly from the definition of the Wright-Fisher model with seed bank in a
fast-changing environment introduced in Section
2.3
, and from Assumption
2
. It remains to establish (
2.49
). By (
5.66
) and (
5.67
), we have for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[x^{N}_{0}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right)-x^{N}_{0}(t)\Bigg|\,\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg]

(5.73)

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{0}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}(t+1/N))x_{0}^{N}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)}{\left((1-x^{N}_{0}(t))+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t)\right)(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}(t+1/N))+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)}-x_{0}^{N}(t).

Applying the tower property
and (
2.46
),
and observing that conditional on
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
, the random variable

{\upsilon}_{0}^{N}(t+1/N)

in (
5.73
) has
distribution given by (
5.68
), we
conclude that (
2.49
)
follows from (
5.69
).
∎

It will be convenient to write the dynamics of the Wright-Fisher model in a
fast-changing environment in such way that we keep track of both
the noise from the binomial sampling and that from the environment. To study the noise arising from the genetic sampling, we introduce for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of i.i.d. random variables
\left\{{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}_{0}\,|\,{\tau}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
taking values in the space of measurable functions from
[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K+1}
to
\mathbb{R}
given by

{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left({\zeta}^{N,{\tau}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})-\frac{b_{0}(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}{\left((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}\right)(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}\right).

(5.74)

To study the noise term in the dynamics of mutant individuals that arises from the environmental fluctuations, we introduce for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K+1}
, the term

\displaystyle{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}_{\textrm{env}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{b_{0}(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}{\left((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}\right)(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}

(5.75)

\displaystyle-\frac{2ps^{2}_{N}(1-x_{0})((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0})\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}\Big)}{\left(h(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})^{2}-s^{2}_{N}(1-(1-b_{0})x_{0})^{2}\right)h(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})}

\displaystyle-\frac{b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}{(1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}},

where the function
h:[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined in (
2.50
).

Remark
.

Observe that, if
\bar{{\upsilon}}
is a random variable with distribution
{\pi}^{N}
defined in (
5.68
) and
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}
, then, by applying identity (
5.69
) with

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i},

(5.76)

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=b_{0}x_{0},

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=(1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i},

\displaystyle f_{4}

\displaystyle=b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i},

and by rearranging terms, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[{\theta}^{N,{\tau}}_{\textrm{env}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\right]=0.

(5.77)

We now rewrite the dynamics of
(x^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in terms of semimartingales and a forcing field. Recall the definition of
F^{\textrm{fast}}_{0}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in (
2.53
). Using (
5.67
), (
5.74
) and (
5.75
), we have that for any
T\in[0,\infty)
and every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle x^{N}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle=x^{N}_{0}(0)+M^{N}_{0}(T)+M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}F^{\textrm{fast}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor

(5.78)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}2p(Ns^{2}_{N})\left(\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0})\left((1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\right)}{(1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}}\right)\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N),

where
(M^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
are càdlàg processes given by, for all
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle M^{N}_{0}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right),

(5.79)

\displaystyle M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}{\theta}^{N,t}_{\textrm{env}}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right).

Our next result concerns the processes
(M^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the processes
M^{N}_{0}
and
{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
are square integrable martingales. Moreover, for all
T\in[0,\infty)
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\Big((M^{N}_{0}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big((M^{N}_{env}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
