are uniformly integrable. Furthermore, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
T\geq 0
,

\left\langle M^{N}_{0},M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right\rangle(T)=0.

(5.80)

Proof.

We shall study the processes
M^{N}_{0}
and
{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
separately.

Step
(i)
: Characterisation of
M^{N}_{0}
:

By the tower property of conditional expectations, and then by (
5.79
), (
5.74
), and (
5.66
), we have that for all
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}(t)\,|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]

(5.81)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}(t)\,\Big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)\right)\right]\,\Big|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

and therefore
(M^{N}_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. For the uniform integrability, we first observe that (
5.79
), (
5.74
), and (
5.66
) imply that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely

\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\;|M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{0}(t)|\leq 1.

(5.82)

Moreover, using the binomial sampling and rearranging terms, for all
t\geq 0
and
\bar{{\upsilon}}\in\{-s_{N},0,s_{N}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(M^{N}_{0}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{0}(t)\Big)^{2}\,\Big|\,{\sigma}\left(\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t},\left\{{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right)=\bar{{\upsilon}}\right\}\right)\right]

(5.83)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\frac{(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})(1-x^{N}_{0}(t))\left(b_{0}(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})x^{N}_{0}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1}^{N}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)\right)}{\left[\Big((1+x^{N}_{0}(t))+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t)\Big)(1+\bar{{\upsilon}})+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)\right]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0}(t))\left(b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1}^{N}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)\right)}{\left[\Big((1+x^{N}_{0}(t))+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t)\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t))x^{N}_{i}(t)\right]^{2}}+\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}}{N}{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N},\bar{{\upsilon}}),

where
{\epsilon}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\sup_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K+1}}|{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\bar{{\upsilon}})|<\infty.

(5.84)

Taking the conditional expectation given
\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}
on both sides of (
5.83
) and then using the tower property and (
2.45
), we conclude that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
T\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{N}_{0}\right\rangle(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N))\left(b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t/N)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t/N))x^{N}_{i}(t/N)\right)}{\left[\left(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N)\right)+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t/N)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t/N))x^{N}_{i}(t/N)\right]^{2}}

(5.85)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2}).

In particular, for all
T\geq 0
, we have that almost surely

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\;\left\langle M^{N}_{0}\right\rangle(T)\leq\frac{T}{4}.

(5.86)

Using (
5.82
) and (
5.86
), we can use Lemma
5.1
to conclude that for all
T\in[0,\infty)
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left(M^{N}_{0}(T)\right)^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable, as desired.

Step
(ii)
: Characterisation of
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
:

The fact that
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
is a martingale follows directly from (
5.77
). Moreover, by (
5.75
), using (
5.70
) and (
5.76
) and rearranging terms, we conclude that
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a predictable finite variation process
(\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
such that

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right\rangle(T)

\displaystyle=2p(Ns_{N}^{2})\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Big(1-x^{N}_{0}\Big)^{2}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\Big)^{2}}{\Big[(1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\Big]^{2}}\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N)+s_{N}\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{N}(T),

(5.87)

and for every
T>0
, there exists
C_{T}^{(1)}>0
such that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely

\sup_{t\leq T}\;|\tilde{{\epsilon}}^{N}(t)|\leq C_{T}^{(1)}.

(5.88)

Moreover, Assumption
2
and (
5.75
) imply that there exists
C^{(2)}_{T}>0
such that almost surely

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}\;\left|M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t+1/N)-M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t)\right|\leq C_{T}^{(2)}.

(5.89)

By (
5.87
), (
5.88
), (
5.89
) and by (
2.45
), we can use Lemma
5.1
to conclude that for all
T\in[0,\infty)
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left(M^{N}_{env}(T)\right)^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

Step
(iii)
: Orthogonality of
M^{N}_{0}
and
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
:

It remains to establish (
5.80
). By (
5.79
), it will suffice to establish that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right){\theta}^{N,t}_{\textrm{env}}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\right]=0.

(5.90)

By (
5.74
) and (
5.75
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right){\theta}^{N,t}_{\textrm{env}}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)

(5.91)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle={\theta}^{N,t}_{\textrm{env}}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=0,

where the last equality follows from (
5.66
) and (
5.74
). Identity (
5.90
) then follows from (
5.91
) and the tower property, which completes the proof.
∎

We will also need to describe the dynamics of
\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}
in terms of a semimartingale and a large drift. Recall the definition of
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
in (
2.53
). For
i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket
, we have

{\upsilon}_{i}^{N}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},i}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor.

Moreover, we have

{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}(T)=\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},0}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor,

(5.92)

where

\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)=\sum_{t=0}^{\lfloor NT\rfloor-1}{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(t+\frac{1}{N}\right).

(5.93)

Next, we characterise the process
(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a square-integrable martingale, and for all
T\in[0,\infty)
, the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)\right)^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable. Moreover, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
T\geq 0
,

\left\langle M^{N}_{0},\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right\rangle(T)=0.

(5.94)

Finally, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a finite variation process
({\epsilon}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
such that for every
T\in[0,\infty)
, there exists
C_{T}>0
for which for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely

\sup_{t\leq T}|{\epsilon}^{N}(t)|\leq C_{T},

and for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{N}_{\textrm{env}},\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right\rangle(T)

(5.95)

\displaystyle=-{2p(Ns^{2}_{N})}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\left(1-x^{N}_{0}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\right)}{\Big[(1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\Big]^{2}}\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N)+s_{N}{\epsilon}^{N}(T).

Proof.

By (
5.93
), (
2.48
) and Assumption
2
,
(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a sum of mean zero i.i.d. random variables, and therefore
(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. By Assumption
2
and (
5.93
), we also have

\left\langle\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right\rangle(T)=2p(Ns^{2}_{N})\frac{\lfloor NT\rfloor}{N}.

(5.96)

Also, by (
2.45
) and (
2.48
), we have that almost surely

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{t\leq T}|\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t)-\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t-)|\leq 1.

(5.97)

Using (
2.45
), (
5.96
), (
5.97
), and then applying Lemma
5.1
, we conclude that the sequences of random variables
\left(\left[\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\Big(\left(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(T)\right)^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable.

It remains to compute the predictable bracket covariation between
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
and the martingales
M^{N}_{0}
and
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
introduced in (
5.79
). Observe that by (
5.79
) and (
5.93
), (
5.94
) will follow after establishing that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\cdot{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\right]=0.

(5.98)

To show that (
5.98
) holds, we observe that by (
5.79
), (
5.74
) and (
5.66
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\cdot{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle={\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[{\theta}^{N,t}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\times{\sigma}\left({\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=0,

and therefore (
5.98
) follows from the tower property of conditional expectation.

It remains to establish (
5.95
). Observe that by (
5.75
) and by using identity (
5.72
) from Lemma
5.6
with (
5.76
), we have that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\cdot{\theta}^{N,t}_{\textrm{env}}\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}\left(\frac{t}{N}\right),{\upsilon}^{N}_{0}\left(\frac{t+1}{N}\right)\right)\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t/N}\right]

(5.99)

\displaystyle=-2ps^{2}_{N}\frac{\left(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N)\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t/N))x^{N}_{i}(t/N)\right)}{\Big[(1-x^{N}_{0}(t/N))+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}(t/N)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1}(t/N)x^{N}_{i}(t/N)\Big]^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(s_{N}^{3}).

Identity (
5.95
) then follows from (
5.99
) and (
2.45
), which completes the proof.
∎

We will also need to characterise the finite-variation process that appears on the right-hand side of (
5.78
).
To simplify notation, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in[0,\infty)
, let

A^{N}_{0}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{0}^{T}2p(Ns^{2}_{N})\left(\frac{(1-x^{N}_{0})\left((1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}\right)}{(1-x^{N}_{0})+b_{0}x^{N}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}^{N}_{i-1})x^{N}_{i}}\right)\,d(\lfloor Nt\rfloor/N).

(5.100)

The next result is an immediate consequence of Assumption
2
. Recall the definition of total variation introduced before Assumption
4
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
T\in[0,\infty)
, the following conditions hold:

(i)

The sequence
\left(\mathfrak{T}(A^{N}_{0},T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable.

(ii)

For any
{\varepsilon}>0
, we have

\lim_{{\delta}\rightarrow 0^{+}}\,\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\leq T}\left(\mathfrak{T}(A^{N}_{0},t+{\delta})-\mathfrak{T}(A^{N}_{0},t)\right)>{\varepsilon}\right)=0.

Proof.

Observe that the integrand on the right-hand side of (
5.100
) is non-negative and therefore for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
T\in[0,\infty)
,
\mathfrak{T}(A^{N}_{0},T)=A^{N}_{0}(T)
. Then, the lemma follows from the observation that by (
2.45
),

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}})\in[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}}2p(Ns^{2}_{N})\left(\frac{(1-x_{0})\left((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}\right)}{(1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}(1+{\upsilon}_{i-1})x_{i}}\right)<\infty.

∎

In order to apply the Katzenberger’s framework to the analysis of the Wright-Fisher model with a fast-changing environment, we will also need to characterise the flow
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
defined in (
2.53
) and its associated projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
introduced after (
2.54
). This will be our next result. Recall the definition of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
in (
2.54
), the definition of the flow
\boldsymbol{F}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
in (
2.12
) and its associated projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
in (
2.16
), and of the set
D(1)
introduced before Assumption
3
.
Recall that we use
\boldsymbol{0}
to denote the
K
-dimensional null vector.

Lemma 5.10
.

For any
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
, the Jacobian of
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})
, i.e. the matrix
\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
, has exactly one eigenvalue
0
and
2K
eigenvalues in
D(1)
. Moreover, for any
\boldsymbol{x}=(x,\cdots,x)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the following relations hold

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}\left(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0}\right)=\frac{1}{B(1-x)+1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}\left(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0}\right)=\frac{(1-b_{0})x(1-x)}{B(1-x)+1}.

(5.101)

Furthermore,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0}\right)=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}(\boldsymbol{x}).

(5.102)

The proof of Lemma
5.10
is based on formulae (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), and on Theorem
3.2
. Since we used a similar argument in Section
3.4
when we studied the properties of the flow arising from the Wright-Fisher model with seed bank in a constant environment, we will postpone the proof of Lemma
5.10
to Section
6.5
.

We are now ready to establish Theorem
2.10
.

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

Our proof consists of an application of
Theorem
3.1
. As in the proof of
Theorem
2.6
, in order to
keep our notation consistent with that of
Theorem
3.1
, we introduce the

(2K+1)
-dimensional process

\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(M^{N}_{0}(t)+M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t)+A^{N}_{0}(t),0,\cdots,0,\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}(t),0,\cdots,0\right)_{t\geq 0},

where
M^{N}_{0}
,
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
,
A^{N}_{0}
and
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
are defined in (
5.79
), (
5.100
) and (
5.93
). By (
2.53
), (
5.78
) and (
5.92
), we can write the dynamics of
(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(t),{\upsilon}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
T\in[0,\infty)
as

(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(T),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(T))=(\boldsymbol{x}^{N}(0),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N}(0))+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N})\,d\lfloor Nt\rfloor.

To improve the readability, we split the proof into steps.

Step (i): tightness of
\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

By Theorem
3.1
, and Lemmas
5.7
,
5.8
,
5.9
and
5.10
, the sequence of processes
\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is relatively compact in
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}\times\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}\right)
. From now on, let
(\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a subsequential limit.
Theorem
3.1
implies that

(\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a diffusion with sample paths in

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}\times\{0\}^{K}
, where
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the
diagonal of the
(K+1)
-dimensional hypercube. Since, in the limit,
coordinates describing the environment vanish, and

x_{0}\equiv x_{1}\equiv\cdots\equiv x_{K}
, it will suffice to describe the
dynamics of
x_{0}
.

Step (ii): characterisation of
(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}

To compute the drift and the noise terms of the corresponding SDE, we
need to characterise the limiting process
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
in terms
of
x_{0}
. With this aim in mind, we shall characterise the limit points of each of the martingales
M^{N}_{0}
,
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
and
\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}^{N}
that appear in the dynamics of the discrete process (see Equations (
5.27
), (
5.28
) and (
5.29
)).

Starting with
M^{N}_{0}
, by using the uniform integrability of the
sequences
\left(\left(M^{N}_{0}(T)\right)^{2}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\left(\left[M^{N}_{0}\right](T)\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
stated in Lemma
5.7
, we conclude that
M^{N}_{0}

converges weakly to a process
(M_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
which is a continuous martingale with respect to the filtration generated by
(\boldsymbol{x}(t),\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, and whose quadratic variation is the (weak) limit of
([M^{N}_{0}])_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. Hence, taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
5.85
) and using the continuity of
M_{0}
, we conclude that the quadratic variation of the martingale
M_{0}
is given by, for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

[M_{0}](T)=\langle M_{0}\rangle(T)=\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,dt.

Therefore, there exists a standard Brownian motion
W_{0}
such that for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

M_{0}(T)=\int_{0}^{T}\sqrt{x_{0}(1-x_{0})}\,dW_{0}(t).

(5.103)

Repeating the same argument for
M^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
, using (
5.87
) instead of (
5.85
), and then applying (
2.45
), we conclude that as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the sequence of processes
(M^{N}_{\textrm{env}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges weakly to a continuous martingale
M_{\textrm{env}}
whose quadratic variation is given by, for all
T\geq 0
,

[M_{\textrm{env}}](T)=\langle M_{\textrm{env}}\rangle(T)=2ps^{2}(1-b_{0})^{2}\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}^{2}(1-x_{0})^{2}\,dt.

(5.104)

Therefore, there exists a standard Brownian motion
W_{\textrm{env}}
such that for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

M_{\textrm{env}}(T)=(2p)^{1/2}s(1-b_{0})\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,dW_{\textrm{env}}(t).

(5.105)

Moreover, by taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
in (
5.80
) and using the uniform integrability conditions stated in Lemma
5.7
, we conclude that the covariation of
M_{0}
and
M_{\textrm{env}}
is identically equal to
0
and therefore, by Knight’s theorem, the Brownian motions
W_{0}
and
W_{\textrm{env}}
are independent.

Applying the argument that we used for
M^{N}_{0}
to the analysis of
\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}
, using Lemma
5.8
and identity (
5.96
), we conclude that
\left(\widetilde{M}^{N}_{\textrm{env}}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges weakly to a continuous martingale
\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}
whose quadratic variation is given by

[\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](T)=\langle\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}\rangle(T)=2ps^{2}T.

(5.106)

By taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
5.95
), using the uniform integrability conditions stated in Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
, and then recalling (
5.104
) and (
5.106
), we conclude that the covariation of
M_{\textrm{env}}
and
\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}
satisfies, for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

[M_{\textrm{env}},\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](T)=-\left([M_{\textrm{env}}](T)[\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](T)\right)^{1/2}=-2ps^{2}(1-b_{0})\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\,dt.

(5.107)

Therefore, by Knight’s theorem and (
5.106
), we have for all
T\in[0,\infty)

\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}(T)=-(2p)^{1/2}sW_{\textrm{env}}(T),

(5.108)

where
W_{\textrm{env}}
is the standard Brownian motion that appears on the right-hand side of (
5.105
).

To compute the drift of the limiting SDE, we also need to characterise the limit of the sequence of finite variation processes
(A^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
defined in (
5.100
). By Lemma
5.9
and by Theorem
3.1
, the sequence
(A^{N}_{0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges weakly to a finite variation process
A_{0}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. By taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
5.100
), and then using dominated convergence and (
2.45
), we conclude that for all
T\in[0,\infty)

A_{0}(T)=2ps^{2}(1-b_{0})\int_{0}^{T}x_{0}(1-x_{0})\Big((1-x_{0})+b_{0}x_{0}\Big)\,dt.

(5.109)

Step (iii): the limiting SDE

We are now ready to state the limiting SDE for
x_{0}
. Applying Theorem
3.1
to the sequence of processes
\left(\boldsymbol{x}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}^{N},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}^{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, we conclude that in the limit,
(x_{0}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies the following SDE

\displaystyle dx_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}dA_{0}(t)+\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}dM_{0}(t)+\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}dM_{\textrm{env}}(t)+\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}d\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}d[M_{0}](t)

(5.110)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}^{2}}d[M_{\textrm{env}}](t)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}^{2}}d[\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](t)+\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}^{2}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\upsilon}_{0}}d[M_{\textrm{env}},\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](t),

where to compute the the terms involving second-order derivatives we used the fact that for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,

[M_{0},M_{\textrm{env}}](T)=[M_{0},\widetilde{M}_{\textrm{env}}](T)=0.

Then, (
2.55
) follows from applying identities (
5.101
), (
5.102
), (
5.103
), (
5.105
), (
5.107
), (
5.108
) and (
5.109
) to (
5.110
) and rearranging terms. This completes the proof.
∎

6
Proof of technical and auxiliary results

6.1
Explicit solution for ODE with
K=1

In this section we apply the method of characteristics to study the flow in (
2.12
) for
K=1
. Recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is the diagonal of
[0,1]^{2}
. We will establish the following result.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
be the map defined in (
2.16
), associated to the flow
\boldsymbol{F}:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
in (
2.12
) with
K=1
. Then, for any
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{0},x_{1})\in[0,1)\times[0,1]
, we have

\frac{(1-b_{0})x_{1}+b_{0}}{(1-x_{0})}-(1-b_{0})\log(1-x_{0})=\frac{(1-b_{0}){\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})+b_{0}}{(1-{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x}))}-(1-b_{0})\log(1-{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})).

(6.1)

Proof.

For
K=1
, the ODE associated to the flow defined in (
2.12
) is reduced to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}x_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1-b_{0})(1-x_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})}{1-(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\frac{d}{dt}x_{1}(t)=x_{0}-x_{1},

(6.2)

since by (
2.1
), we have
b_{1}=1-b_{0}
. Setting then

y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1-x_{0}\quad\textrm{and}\quad y_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(1-b_{0})x_{1}+b_{0},

(6.3)

we have by (
6.2
) that

\frac{d}{dt}y_{0}(t)=y_{0}\frac{(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})}{1-(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\frac{d}{dt}y_{1}(t)=(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1}).

(6.4)

Observe that
y_{0}
represents the proportion of wild type individuals in the current generation, and
y_{1}
represents the proportion of seeds produced one generation in the past that will contribute to the genetic pool of the current generation. Then, by the chain rule and (
6.4
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dy_{0}}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})}{y_{0}}

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{y_{1}}{y_{0}}+(1-b_{0}),

where for the last equality we used the fact that by (
6.3
),

(1-b_{0})(x_{0}-x_{1})=1-y_{1}-(1-b_{0})y_{0}.

To explicitly solve (
6.5
), we observe that again by the chain rule

\frac{d}{d(\log y_{0})}y_{1}=y_{1}+(1-b_{0})e^{\log y_{0}},

which admits as its unique solution

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=(1-b_{0})(\log y_{0})e^{\log y_{0}}+Ce^{\log y_{0}}

\displaystyle=(1-b_{0})y_{0}\log y_{0}+Cy_{0},

where
C
is a parameter which is determined by the initial condition of the ODE in (
6.5
). Then, by applying (
6.3
), and letting the initial condition be
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{0},x_{1})\in[0,1)\times[0,1]
, we conclude that for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\frac{(1-b_{0})x_{1}+b_{0}}{(1-x_{0})}-(1-b_{0})\log(1-x_{0})=\frac{(1-b_{0})x_{1}(t)+b_{0}}{(1-x_{0}(t))}-(1-b_{0})\log(1-x_{0}(t)).

(6.6)

By Lemma
3.4
, the projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
defined in (
2.16
) is well defined, and moreover we have
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{1}
. Therefore, taking the limit as
t\rightarrow\infty
on the right-hand side of (
6.6
) completes the proof.
∎

One can then establish (
2.21
) by differentiating both sides of (
6.1
) with respect to
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
. We were not able to apply the method of characteristics for the case
K\geq 2
. In particular, we did not find
the analogue of (
6.5
) in that case.

6.2
Proof of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3

In this section, we will prove Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
, i.e. we will establish an explicit formula for the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
which is a quadratic approximation for the stable nonlinear manifold (see (
3.6
)), by studying the dynamical system near the manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
. Recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, defined in (
3.5
), is the invariant attractor manifold of ODE (
3.4
). Suppose Assumptions
3
and
5
are both satisfied. For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, let
\boldsymbol{u}\equiv\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x})
be a right eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated to the eigenvalue
0
, and let
\boldsymbol{v}\equiv\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})
be a left eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated to the eigenvalue
0
, such that
\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=1
. For any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, let
E_{c}\equiv E_{c}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
E_{s}\equiv E_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
denote, respectively, the linear centre and stable manifolds. Observe that
E_{c}=\spanvector(\boldsymbol{u})
, and the right eigenvectors of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated to the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
with negative real part are a basis for
E_{s}
. Recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
is a non-linear centre manifold, and let
\mathfrak{W}_{s}\equiv\mathfrak{W}_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
be the non-linear stable manifold tangent to
E_{s}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
. Also, recall the definition of the projection matrices
\mathcal{P}_{c}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s}
in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), respectively. By Lemma 5.2 in
[
40
]
,
\mathcal{P}_{c}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s}
are well defined and satisfy the relations
\mathcal{P}^{2}_{c}=\mathcal{P}_{c}
,
\mathcal{P}^{2}_{s}=\mathcal{P}_{s}
and
\mathcal{P}_{c}\mathcal{P}_{s}=\mathcal{P}_{s}\mathcal{P}_{c}=0
.

We begin by observing that for any
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
, the left eigenvector
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{x})
is orthogonal to the linear stable manifold
E_{s}(\boldsymbol{x})
. Indeed, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in E_{s}
, there exists
\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\in E_{s}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{y}
, since
E_{s}
is the space spanned by the (generalised) eigenvectors associated to the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
with negative real part. Therefore, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in E_{s}
, there exists
\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}}\in E_{s}
such that

\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{y}},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0.

Moreover
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}=0
, since

\langle\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{v},\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{P}_{s}\left(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}\right),\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=0,

where the last equality is derived by observing that
\mathcal{P}_{s}\left(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\in E_{s}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}
is orthogonal to
E_{s}
.
Following
[
40
]
, we will write the dynamics of the flow given by ODE (
3.4
) in terms of its projections on
E_{c}
and
E_{s}
.

To simplify notation, let
(\boldsymbol{y}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the flow curve of the ODE started from
\boldsymbol{y}(0)\in\mathscr{U}
. Let
{\varepsilon}>0
be such that whenever
\boldsymbol{y}(0)\in\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})\cap\mathfrak{W}_{s}
, we have
\boldsymbol{y}(t)\in\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})\cap\mathfrak{W}_{s}
for all
t\geq 0
. By the smoothness of
\mathbf{F}
and assuming
{\varepsilon}
sufficiently small, we can consider the Taylor expansion of
\mathbf{F}
, and write that for any flow
(\boldsymbol{y}(t))_{t\geq 0}\subset\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})\cap\mathfrak{W}_{s}
,

\frac{d\boldsymbol{y}(t)}{dt}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})+o(||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}||_{2}^{2}),

(6.7)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\left(\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},i}\right)_{i=0}^{K}:\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
is given for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
by

\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},i}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\langle\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x},\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})\Big\rangle.

(6.8)

We shall now compute the impact of the flow dynamics on the projections of
(\boldsymbol{y}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. We start by considering the evolution of the projection onto the linear centre manifold
E_{c}
.

\displaystyle\frac{d\left(\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{x})\right)}{dt}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})+o(||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}||_{2}^{2}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle\boldsymbol{u}

(6.9)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle\boldsymbol{u}+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle\boldsymbol{u}+o(||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}||_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle\boldsymbol{u}+o(||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}||_{2}^{2}),

where the last equality comes from the fact that by construction
\boldsymbol{v}
is a left eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated to the eigenvalue
0
. To compute the derivative of the projection onto the linear stable manifold, we combine the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{s}
in (
3.10
) with identities (
6.7
) and (
6.9
), to obtain

\frac{d\left(\mathcal{P}_{s}(\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{x})\right)}{dt}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x})-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle\boldsymbol{u}+o(||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}||_{2}^{2}).

(6.10)

To simplify notation, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, we shall write
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}=\mathcal{P}_{s}\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}\right)
. By the Centre Manifold Theorem, there is a map
V:E_{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}\cap\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})
if and only if

V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s})\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}).

(6.11)

Then, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}\cap\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})
, we have

\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s})\boldsymbol{u}.

(6.12)

Note that since
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}
, we must have
V(0)=0
. Moreover, as
\mathfrak{W}_{s}
is tangent to
E_{s}
at
\boldsymbol{x}
, we have
\nabla V(0)=0
. Hence, by Taylor expanding
V
around
0
, we find that for any
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
with
||\boldsymbol{y}_{s}||_{2}
sufficiently small,

V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s})=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle+o(||\boldsymbol{y}_{s}||_{2}^{2}),

(6.13)

where
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is a symmetric square matrix of order
K+1
. Comparing (
6.13
) to (
3.6
), the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is the one that we aim to compute. Since the domain of
V
is the stable linear manifold
E_{s}
, we may assume without loss of generality that

\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}.

(6.14)

Furthermore, by (
6.11
), the invariance of the non-linear manifold
\mathfrak{W}_{s}
under the flow implies that if
\boldsymbol{y}(0)\in\mathfrak{W}_{s}\cap\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{x},{\varepsilon})
, then for all
t\geq 0
,

V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}(t))\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{x}).

(6.15)

Differentiating both sides of (
6.15
) with respect to time, we conclude by the chain rule that

\left\langle\nabla V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}(t)),\frac{d\boldsymbol{y}_{s}(t)}{dt}\right\rangle\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{d\left(\mathcal{P}_{c}(\boldsymbol{y}(t)-\boldsymbol{x})\right)}{dt}.

(6.16)

By applying (
6.9
), (
6.10
), (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) to (
6.16
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\Big\langle\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\Big(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s})\boldsymbol{u}\Big)\Big\rangle+\mathcal{O}(||\boldsymbol{y}_{s}||_{2}^{3})=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+V(\boldsymbol{y}_{s})\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle+o(||\boldsymbol{y}_{s}||_{2}^{2}).

Recalling that
\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)
, identity (
6.8
), and equating the quadratic terms, we conclude that for any
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
, we must have

\Big\langle\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\Big\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle.

(6.17)

We now show that one can obtain
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
from a linear system.

Lemma 6.2
.

The matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
satisfies (
3.11
).

Proof.

We start by recalling (
6.8
), and rewriting the right-hand side of (
6.17
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}),\boldsymbol{v}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Big\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle,

where we used the fact that
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}=\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}
since
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
to derive the third and fourth equalities. Hence, by (
6.17
) and since
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is symmetric, we conclude that for all
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
,

\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle=0.

(6.18)

We shall verify that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}
is skew-symmetric. Recall that a square matrix
\mathbf{M}
of order
K+1
is skew-symmetric if and only if
\mathbf{M}=-\mathbf{M}^{*}
, or, equivalently, if and only if
\langle\boldsymbol{y},\mathbf{M}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle=0
, for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
. By the definition of the projections
\mathcal{P}_{c}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s}
, for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, there exists
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
such that
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u}
. Hence, by the bilinearity of the inner product,

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{y},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{y}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u}),\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+r\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}_{s},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+r\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+r^{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle.

Applying (
6.18
) and the fact that
\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)\cap\ker\left(\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{y},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{y}\right\rangle

(6.19)

\displaystyle=r\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=r\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=r\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{u},\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)\boldsymbol{x}_{s}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

where we applied the identity
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}=\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
to derive the second equality. Since (
6.19
) holds for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}
is skew-symmetric. Therefore,

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}=-\left(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}F_{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\right)^{*}.

(6.20)

Recalling that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
and
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})
are symmetric for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket_{0}
and by rearranging the terms in (
6.20
), our claim holds.
∎

By Lemma
6.2
, it will suffice to solve Equation (
3.11
) to compute
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
. Existence and uniqueness of solutions depend in general on the eigenvalues of the Jacobian matrix
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}

[
11
, Theorem VII.2.1]
. If all the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
had negative real part, then we would classify Equation (
3.11
) as a stable Lyapunov equation, and existence and uniqueness of solutions would be immediate. Since
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
has one eigenvalue
0
, and all the other eigenvalues with negative real part, we say this is a singular Lyapunov equation which is semistable (see Section 4 in
[
9
]
as a reference on this theme). In this case, it is not clear
a priori
that existence and uniqueness hold. This will be our next result.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists a unique square matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
of order
K+1
which satisfies both Equations (
3.11
) and (
6.14
).

Proof.

Note that a square matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
satisfies (
6.14
) if, and only if, it is an element of the real vector space

\mathscr{V}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\varphi}\in\mathscr{L}\left(\mathbb{R}^{K+1},\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\right):\;E_{c}\subset\ker({\varphi})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}{\varphi}={\varphi}\right\},

where for any vector spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is the set of
\mathcal{Y}
-valued linear maps defined on
\mathcal{X}
. We now define the linear operators
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}},\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}:\mathscr{V}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}
by, for all
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{V}
,

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}({\varphi})=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}{\varphi}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}({\varphi})={\varphi}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}.

Since
E_{c}=\ker\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)
, we have
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathcal{P}_{s}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
, and therefore the operators
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
are well defined. Let
L={\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}+\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
. To prove our claim, it will be enough to establish that
L
is invertible, i.e. that
0\not\in\spec(L)
.

Observing that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
commute, i.e. that for any
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{V}
, we have

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}{\varphi}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}({\varphi}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}})=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}{\varphi}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}{\varphi})=\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}{\varphi},

we conclude that
\spec(L)\subseteq\spec({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}})+\spec(\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})
(see for instance
[
33
, Theorem 2.4.9]
for a proof of this fact). Hence, it will suffice to prove that
0\not\in\spec({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}})+\spec(\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})
. To verify this, observe that we can extend
\mathscr{V}
to a complex vector space by taking

\widehat{\mathscr{V}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\varphi}\in\mathscr{L}\left(\mathbb{C}^{K+1},\mathbb{C}^{K+1}\right):\;E_{c}\subset\ker({\varphi})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}{\varphi}={\varphi}\right\}.

Then
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}},\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}\in\mathscr{L}(\widehat{\mathscr{V}},\widehat{\mathscr{V}})
. Let
{\lambda}
be an eigenvalue of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}
, and let
{\varsigma}\neq 0
be an associated eigenvector. Then for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{K+1}
, we have

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y}=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}({\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y})={\lambda}({\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y}).

Hence,
{\lambda}
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}
, which means by Assumption
3
that either
{\lambda}=0
or
\Re({\lambda})<0
. We now claim that only the latter alternative is possible. Indeed, if
{\lambda}=0
, we would have that for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{K+1}
there should exist
r\in\mathbb{C}
such that
{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y}=r\boldsymbol{v}
. Since
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}{\varsigma}={\varsigma}
, this would imply that for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{K+1}
,

{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y}=\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{y}=r\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}\boldsymbol{v}=0,

which is a contradiction since
{\varsigma}\neq 0
. Now, let
\tilde{{\lambda}}
be an eigenvalue of
\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}
, and let
\tilde{{\varsigma}}
be an associated eigenvector. Then for all
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{K+1}
we have

\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}}\tilde{{\varsigma}}\boldsymbol{y}=\tilde{{\lambda}}\tilde{{\varsigma}}\boldsymbol{y}=\tilde{{\varsigma}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{y}.

Suppose that
\tilde{{\lambda}}\not\in\spec\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Then, from the previous identity, we conclude that for any eigenvector
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}
of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
associated with an eigenvalue
{\lambda}
with negative real part, we must have

(\tilde{{\lambda}}-{\lambda})\tilde{{\varsigma}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}=0,

which implies that
\tilde{{\varsigma}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}=0
. Since by construction
\tilde{{\varsigma}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
, we conclude that
\tilde{{\varsigma}}=0
, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\spec(\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})\subseteq\spec\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)\setminus\{0\}
. Finally, by Assumption
3
and our previous arguments, this means that
{\lambda}\in\spec({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}})\cup\spec(\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})\Rightarrow\Re({\lambda})<0
. In particular,
0\not\in\spec(L)=\spec({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}+\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})\subset\spec({\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}})+\spec(\widetilde{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}})
, and therefore the operator
L
is invertible, as desired.
∎

Lemma
6.3
shows that we can obtain the matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
by solving a system of linear equations. Theorem
3.2
can be then understood as a corollary of Lemma
6.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem
3.2
follows from Lemma
6.3
and the observation that a symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
of order
K+1
satisfies both Equations (
6.14
) and (
3.11
) if, and only if,
it satisfies both
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
and (
3.11
). In fact, if
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
satisfies (
6.14
), then
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{u}=0
. On the other hand, if
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
, then for all
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbf{R}^{K+1}
, we have

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}_{s}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+r\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
implies that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}=\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{P}_{s}
. Since
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is symmetric, this implies that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}=\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
, i.e. that identity (
6.14
) is satisfied, as desired.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

To prove our desired claim, we first show that
the integral expression for
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is well defined, for which it will suffice to establish that there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\max\Big\{\Big|\Big|\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Big|\Big|,\,\Big|\Big|\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\Big|\Big|\Big\}\leq C_{1}e^{-C_{2}t},

(6.21)

where
||\cdot||
denotes the operator norm. In order to establish (
6.21
), observe that for any
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle\boldsymbol{u}
, where
\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\in E_{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\Big|\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})(\boldsymbol{y}_{s}+\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle\boldsymbol{u})\Big|\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\Big|\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\Big|\Big|+|\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle|\cdot\Big|\Big|\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{u}\Big|\Big|

\displaystyle\leq||\mathcal{P}_{s}||\cdot\Big|\Big|\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{y}_{s}\Big|\Big|+|\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle|\cdot||\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{u}||

\displaystyle\leq e^{-Ct}||\mathcal{P}_{s}||\cdot||\boldsymbol{y}_{s}||,

where in the second inequality we used the fact that since
\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker\left(\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)
,
\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{u}\equiv\boldsymbol{u}
for all
t\geq 0
, and in the final one we used the fact that on
E_{s}
, all the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
have negative real part (see
[
47
]
). Observing that, since
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}\boldsymbol{v}=0
,
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{s}
is a projection matrix on the space spanned by the eigenvectors of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}
associated to the eigenvalues with negative real part, we can also bound
\Big|\Big|\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Big|\Big|
. Hence, (
6.21
) is proved, and therefore the expression
for
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is well defined. Symmetry of the Hessian implies that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is a
symmetric matrix. Moreover,

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{u}=0
, since

\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{u}=0.

By Theorem
6.3
, it remains to check that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
also solves Equation (
3.11
). Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s},

since, by (
6.21
),

\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})=0.

Therefore, the formula holds.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\succeq 0
. Fix
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, and consider the ODE

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\boldsymbol{y}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{y}(t),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}(0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{y},

with solution
\boldsymbol{y}(t)=\exp({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t})\boldsymbol{y}
, for all
t\geq 0
. By the integral representation of
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}\boldsymbol{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\boldsymbol{y}^{*}\exp\Big({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{*}t}\Big)\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\exp\Big({\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}t}\Big)\boldsymbol{y}\,dt

(6.22)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left\langle\boldsymbol{y}(t),\,\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}\boldsymbol{y}(t)\right\rangle\;dt

\displaystyle\leq 0,

since we assumed
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}v_{i}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{i})\right)\mathcal{P}_{s}
is positive semidefinite. Since (
6.22
) holds for any
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}
is negative semidefinite, which completes the proof.
∎

6.3
Proof of Lemma
5.1

We will need the following version of the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy (BDG) inequality for càdlàg martingales; the inequality in this form is stated on page 527 of
[
43
]
, and in (53) in
[
25
]
, and it is a consequence of
[
19
, Theorem 21.1]
. For a square-integrable càdlàg martingale
(M(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
M(0)=0
, for
T\geq 0
and
p\geq 2
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\leq T}\left|M(t)\right|^{p}\right]\lesssim_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\langle M\right\rangle(T)\right)^{p/2}+\sup_{t\leq T}\left|M(t)-M(t-)\right|^{p}\right],

(6.23)

where
(\langle M\rangle(t))_{t\geq 0}
denotes the unique predictable càdlàg process for which
\left(M^{2}(t)-\langle M\rangle(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale.

We are ready to prove Lemma
5.1
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

The fact that the sequence of random variables
\Big((M^{N}(T))^{2}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable follows directly from the conditions of the lemma and an application of the BDG inequality in (
6.23
) with
p=4
. By the standard BDG inequality and (
6.23
), we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big[M^{N}\Big]^{p/2}(T)\Big]\lesssim_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\langle M\right\rangle(T)\right)^{p/2}+\sup_{t\leq T}\left|M(t)-M(t-)\right|^{p}\right],

and therefore, by the conditions (i) and (ii) of this lemma, the sequence of random variables
\Big(\left[M^{N}\right](T)\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is also uniformly integrable, which completes the proof.
∎

6.4
Proof of Lemma
5.6

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Observe that by an algebraic manipulation of the variables, we have

\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}=\frac{f_{1}}{f_{3}}-\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}^{2}f_{4}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{(f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4})f_{3}^{2}}.

(6.24)

By taking expectations with respect to
\bar{{\upsilon}}
on both sides of (
6.24
), and then using (
5.68
) and the fact that

\frac{1}{(f_{3}+s_{N}f_{4})}+\frac{1}{(f_{3}-s_{N}f_{4})}=\frac{2f_{3}}{(f_{3}^{2}-s_{N}^{2}f_{4}^{2})},

we conclude that (
5.69
) holds.

In order to establish (
5.70
) and (
5.71
), we observe that by combining (
6.24
) with (
5.69
), we have for every
q\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}-\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]\right)^{q}

(6.25)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}^{2}f_{4}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}(f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4})}-\frac{2ps_{N}^{2}f_{4}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{(f_{3}^{2}-s_{N}^{2}f_{4}^{2})f_{3}}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{q}\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}^{q}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})^{q}}{f_{3}^{2q}}

\displaystyle+(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})^{q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1}+l_{2}+l_{3}=q:\\
l_{2}+l_{3}>0\end{subarray}}\bar{{\upsilon}}^{l_{1}+2l_{2}}s_{N}^{2l_{3}}\left(\frac{(-1)^{l_{1}+l_{3}}(2p)^{l_{3}}q!f_{4}^{l_{2}+l_{3}}}{l_{1}!l_{2}!l_{3}!f_{3}^{q+l_{1}+l_{2}}(f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}})^{l_{2}}(f_{3}^{2}-s_{N}^{2}f_{4}^{2})^{l_{3}}}\right).

Identities (
5.70
) and (
5.71
) then follow by taking expectations on both sides of (
6.25
) and using (
5.68
).

It remains to establish (
5.72
). By using (
5.68
) and then (
6.24
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\bar{{\upsilon}}\left(\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}-\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{f_{1}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{2}}{f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4}}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\pi}^{N}}\left[\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{1}}{f_{3}}-\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}^{2}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}}+\frac{\bar{{\upsilon}}^{3}f_{4}(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{(f_{3}+\bar{{\upsilon}}f_{4})f_{3}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=-2ps_{N}^{2}\frac{(f_{1}f_{4}-f_{2}f_{3})}{f_{3}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(s_{N}^{3}),

where the last equality also holds due to (
5.68
), which completes the proof.
∎

6.5
Proof of Lemmas
2.11
and
5.10

We will start by establishing Lemma
5.10
. Recall the definition of the flow
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times(-1,1)^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
in (
2.53
) and its associated projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
introduced after (
2.54
). Recall the definition of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
in (
2.54
), the definition of
\boldsymbol{F}:[0,1]^{K+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K+1}
in (
2.12
) and its associated projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}
in (
2.16
). Also, recall the definition of
D(1)
introduced before Assumption
3
.

Our strategy will be to use (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) to compute the derivatives of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}
in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
. In the remainder of this section, let
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})=(x,\cdots,x,0,\cdots,0)\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
. Observe that the Jacobian of
\boldsymbol{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}

(6.26)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}-(1-b_{0})(1-x)&b_{1}(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)&b_{1}x(1-x)&b_{2}x(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}x(1-x)\\
1&-1&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&-1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&-1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&1&-1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&-1\end{array}\right).

Next, we characterise the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
, and show that Assumption
3
is satisfied in the case of a fast-changing environment.

Lemma 6.4
.

For every
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
, the Jacobian matrix of the flow

\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})
has exactly one eigenvalue equal to
0
and
2K
eigenvalues in the open disk

D(1)=\{{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:\;|{\lambda}+1|<1\}
.

Proof.

We will follow closely the proof of Lemma
3.4
. Observe that
{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
if and only
{\lambda}+1
is an
eigenvalue of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}+\mathbf{I}_{2K+1}
,
where
\mathbf{I}_{2K+1}
is the identity matrix of order
2K+1
. By using (
6.26
) and observing that the bottom-right
K\times K
principal submatrix of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
is lower triangular, and then by directly computing the characteristic polynomial
\tilde{p}^{*}
of

\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}+\mathbf{I}_{2K+1}
, we conclude that the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
are the solutions of the polynomial equation

\tilde{p}^{*}({\lambda}+1)=(-1)^{K}({\lambda}+1)^{K}\tilde{p}({\lambda}+1),

(6.27)

where
\tilde{p}
is the polynomial defined in (
3.15
). We proved in
Lemma
3.4
that

{\lambda}=0
is a root of algebraic multiplicity one of
\tilde{p}({\lambda}+1)=0
, all the other roots of
\tilde{p}({\lambda}+1)=0
have negative real part and are elements of the disk
D(1)
. Hence, (
6.27
) implies that
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
has exactly one eigenvalue
0
and
2K
eigenvalues in
D(1)
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
6.4
implies that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
is the attractor manifold for the flow in (
2.53
), and therefore the projection map
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}:[0,1]^{K+1}\times[-1,1]^{K}
introduced after (
2.54
) is well defined. Let
{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
be a right eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
associated with the eigenvalue
0
. To simplify computations, we set

{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
1\\
\vdots\\
1\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{array}\right).

(6.28)

Let
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}\equiv{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2K+1}
denote the left eigenvector of
\mathcal{J}\mathbf{F}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}
associated with the eigenvalue
0
for which
\langle{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\,\textrm{fast}},{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}\rangle=1
. This gives

{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}=\frac{1}{(B(1-x)+1)}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
(1-b_{0})(1-x)\\
\vdots\\
b_{K}(1-x)\\
(1-b_{0})x(1-x)\\
(\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i})x(1-x)\\
\vdots\\
b_{K}x(1-x)\end{array}\right),

(6.29)

where
B
is defined in (
2.3
). Observe that the first
K
entries of
{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}

coincide with those of the right (
3.16
)
and left (
3.17
) eigenvectors, respectively,
associated with the eigenvalue
0
of the Jacobian of the flow
\boldsymbol{F}

arising in the Wright-Fisher dynamics under a constant environment. We also parametrise the manifold
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
using the map
{\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}:[0,1]\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
given by

{\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(x,x,\cdots,x,0,0,\cdots,0)^{*}.

(6.30)

Observe that the map
{\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
satisfies

\frac{d}{dx}{\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(x)=(1,1,\cdots,1,0,0,\cdots,0)^{*}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}{\boldsymbol{{\gamma}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}(x)=0.

(6.31)

Next, we compute the first order derivatives of the map
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}_{0}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\boldsymbol{b}=(b_{i})_{i=0}^{K}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
, and any
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
, Equation (
5.101
) holds.

Proof.

The result follows from using (
3.7
) with (
6.29
), (
6.30
) and (
6.31
).
∎

We are now in a position to establish Lemma
5.10
.

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

By Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
, it remains only to verify that (
5.102
) holds. To do so we apply Theorem
3.2
.

Step (i): Projection onto the linear stable manifold.

Using (
6.28
) and (
6.29
) in the definition (
3.10
), we compute the projection matrix

\mathcal{P}_{s}^{\,\textrm{fast}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{s}&\boldsymbol{R}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{K\times(K+1)}&\boldsymbol{I}_{K}\end{pmatrix},\qquad(\mathcal{P}_{s}^{\,\textrm{fast}})^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}&\boldsymbol{0}_{(K+1)\times K}\\
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}&\boldsymbol{I}_{K}\end{pmatrix},

(6.32)

where
\mathcal{P}_{s}
is defined in (
3.20
).
The matrix
\boldsymbol{R}
has the form

\boldsymbol{R}=x(1-x)\begin{pmatrix}-(1-b_{0})&-\!\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}&\cdots&-b_{K}\\
-(1-b_{0})&-\!\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}&\cdots&-b_{K}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-(1-b_{0})&-\!\sum_{i=2}^{K}b_{i}&\cdots&-b_{K}\end{pmatrix}.

Step (ii): Hessian of the fast-environment flow.

From (
2.53
), the Hessians of
F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{i}
vanish along
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
for all
i\in\llbracket K\rrbracket
, and similarly
\Hess(F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\textrm{env},i})\equiv 0
for
i\in\llbracket K-1\rrbracket
. Therefore, at
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
, the only non-zero Hessian block is

\Hess_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}\!\left(F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})&\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}\\
\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}^{*}&\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}\end{pmatrix},

(6.33)

where the entries of
\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})
are given by (
3.26
), while
\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}
are given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}2b_{1}(1-b_{0})x(1-x)-b_{1}x&2b_{2}(1-b_{0})x(1-x)-b_{2}x&\cdots&2b_{K}(1-b_{0})x(1-x)-b_{K}x\\
b_{1}(1-x)-2b_{1}^{2}x(1-x)&-2b_{1}b_{2}x(1-x)&\cdots&-2b_{1}b_{K}x(1-x)\\
-2b_{1}b_{2}x(1-x)&b_{2}(1-x)-2b_{2}^{2}x(1-x)&\cdots&-2b_{2}b_{K}x(1-x)\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-2b_{1}b_{K}x(1-x)&-2b_{2}b_{K}x(1-x)&\cdots&b_{K}(1-x)-2b_{K}^{2}x(1-x)\end{array}\right),

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}_{\boldsymbol{{\upsilon},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}}=-2x^{2}(1-x)\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}b_{1}^{2}&b_{1}b_{2}&\cdots&b_{1}b_{K}\\
b_{1}b_{2}&b_{2}^{2}&\cdots&b_{2}b_{K}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{1}b_{K}&b_{2}b_{K}&\cdots&b_{K}^{2}\end{array}\right).

Step (iii): Quadratic term of the non-linear stable manifold.

Let

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}&\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}\\
(\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}})^{*}&\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}},\boldsymbol{{\upsilon}}}\end{pmatrix}

denote the quadratic approximation to the stable manifold of the flow
\boldsymbol{F}^{\textrm{fast}}
.
Substituting (
6.26
) and the matrices computed above into the Lyapunov system (
3.11
) together with the constraint (
3.12
), we find that the block

\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}
satisfies

\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}+\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}\mathcal{J}\boldsymbol{F}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=v_{0}\,\mathcal{P}_{s}^{*}\Hess_{\boldsymbol{x}}(F_{0})\mathcal{P}_{s},\qquad\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{u}=0,

where
\boldsymbol{F}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
,
v_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{s}

refer to the constant-environment system
(see (
2.12
), (
3.16
), (
3.17
) and (
3.20
)).
By Theorem
3.2
,
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}
is therefore exactly the curvature matrix for the constant-environment Wright–Fisher flow.

Step (iv): Identifying the second-order derivatives.

Finally, Lemmas
3.5
and
6.5

show that, along
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 0\relax}}}
,

\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})=\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}x_{0}}(\boldsymbol{x}).

Combining this identity with (
3.8
) and Step (iii) yields (
5.102
).
This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to compute the second order derivatives for the case
K=1
.

Proof of Lemma
2.11
.

For
K=1
, by substituting the matrices
\mathcal{P}_{s}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
and
\Hess_{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{0})}\!\left(F^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0}\right)
defined in (
6.32
) and (
6.33
) into the Lyapunov system (
3.11
) together with the constraint (
3.12
), we conclude that the curvature matrix
\boldsymbol{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}}^{\,\textrm{fast}}
satisfies

{\theta}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{0,{\upsilon}_{0}}=\frac{(1-b_{0})^{2}(1-x)^{2}\Big[(1-b_{0})^{2}x^{2}-(1-b_{0})(4-b_{0})x+(2-b_{0})\Big]}{((1-b_{0})(1-x)+1)^{3}((1-b_{0})(1-x)+2)},

(6.34)

and

{\theta}^{\,\textrm{fast}}_{{\upsilon}_{0},{\upsilon}_{0}}=-\frac{(1-b_{0})^{2}x(1-x)^{2}\Big[(1-b_{0})^{2}x^{2}-(1-b_{0})(5-b_{0})x+(4-b_{0})\Big]}{((1-b_{0})(1-x)+1)^{3}((1-b_{0})(1-x)+2)}.

(6.35)

The lemma follows from substituting (
5.101
), (
6.29
), (
6.34
) and (
6.35
) into (
3.8
). Since the proof follows standard algebraic manipulations, we omit the details.
∎
